Under the term *mandinga* — the Cuban and Antillean equivalent of "[Mandé](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mand%C3%A9_peoples)" or "Manding" — were grouped Africans of [Mande](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mande_languages)-speaking origin from [Senegambia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Senegambia), [Guinea-Bissau](12186.md), [Guinea](12176.md), and the regions of the upper [Niger](21437.md): [Wolof](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wolof_people), [Mandinka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandinka_people), [Bambara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bambara_people), [Soninké](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soninke_people), [Fulani](102383.md) (Fula/Peul), and related groups. They arrived primarily during the seventeenth century and the first half of the eighteenth, a period when Senegambia was one of the principal catchment areas of the Atlantic slave trade. A notable characteristic of this group is that a substantial proportion of its members were [Muslim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim) — in some cases with formal religious education in [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic) — which distinguished them from Africans of indigenous religious tradition and from those already partially Christianised in the Kongo. In Cuba and the broader Caribbean, Muslim Mandingas were sometimes identified by their prayer practices, the use of amulets containing Quranic texts (*tilas* or *resguardos*), abstention from pork, and literacy in Arabic. Although the pressures of the slave system and forced evangelisation largely extinguished these practices across generations, scholars including [Sylviane Diouf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sylviane_Diouf) — in her study *Servants of Allah* (1998) — have documented traces of Islamic practice among enslaved Africans in Cuba and elsewhere in the Americas well into the nineteenth century.[^cite_note-diouf1998-176]

#### Mina (Akan and peoples of the Gold Coast)

The term *mina* — derived from [Fort São Jorge da Mina (Elmina)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elmina), in present-day [Ghana](12067.md), the principal Portuguese base on the [Gold Coast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gold_Coast_\(region\)) — designated in Cuba and the Hispanic Caribbean Africans from the Gold Coast, principally speakers of [Akan languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kwa_languages): [Fante](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fante_people), [Asante](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashanti_people), [Akyem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akyem), and neighbouring groups.[^cite_note-177][^cite_note-178] They arrived principally during the eighteenth century, coinciding with the expansion of the [Asante Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashanti_Empire) and the conflicts it generated in the region.[^cite_note-179][^cite_note-180]

The mina in Cuba did not produce a religious tradition as cohesive or identifiable as those of the lucumí or the congos, and their practices were absorbed into the broader substrate of Afro-Cuban religiosity. Some scholars nonetheless note that elements of the Akan religious tradition – including cults to the *[abosom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abosom)* (nature spirits) and distinctive divinatory practices – may be traceable in the syncretic-ritual components of certain Cuban communities, though these are difficult to distinguish from Yoruba contributions given the cultural and geographical proximity of both traditions in West Africa.

#### Other groups: Fulani, Wolof, and Makua

In addition to the principal groups described above, Cuban colonial records document smaller numbers of individuals and communities from other African regions. The [Fula](102383.md) (Fulani or Peul), semi-nomadic pastoralists distributed across [West](67393.md) and Central Africa, arrived principally as captives of the [jihadist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jihadist) wars of the eighteenth and nineteenth centuries in the western [Sahel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sahel); like the Mandingas, many were Muslim.[^cite_note-181][^cite_note-182] The series of Fula jihads that swept the region from [Futa Jallon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Futa_Jallon) and [Futa Toro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Futa_Toro) to the [Sokoto Caliphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sokoto_Caliphate) — beginning in 1725 and continuing into the 1860s – generated large numbers of war captives, many of whom entered the Atlantic slave trade through networks documented by scholars of Muslim identity in the African diaspora.[^cite_note-183] The [Wolof](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wolof_people), originating from [Senegambia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Senegambia), were present primarily in the earliest periods of the trade and left some onomastic traces in Cuban colonial archives. David Wheat's study of Upper Guinean captives in the early Spanish Caribbean demonstrates that Senegambian groups, including the Wolof, formed a substantial proportion of enslaved arrivals in [Havana](49719.md) and other ports during the period 1570–1640, before the trade shifted toward [Angola](701.md) and the [Bight of Benin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bight_of_Benin).[^cite_note-184][^cite_note-185] At the opposite geographical extreme, individuals designated *macuas* or *mozambiques* are documented in Cuba, originating from East Africa – principally from the [Makua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Makua_people) region of northern [Mozambique](19301.md) and southern [Tanzania](30118.md); their presence, though limited in scale, was more significant in Brazil and Cuba than in other Euro-American colonies, primarily through the slave trade operated by Portuguese and Brazilian traffickers during the nineteenth century.[^cite_note-186]

### Multiracial Cubans



Cuban women attend a meeting with U.S. Second Lady Jill Biden during her official visit to Cuba, October 2016.

In the 2012 census, 26.6% of the population (2.97 million people) identified as [mulato](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mulato) or [mestizo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mestizo).[^cite_note-187] In Cuban usage, the term *mestizo* functions as a broad demographic and social category encompassing anyone of mixed racial ancestry, and is not restricted—as it largely is throughout continental Latin America—to persons of specifically indigenous and European descent. The process of [mestizaje](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mestizaje)—continuous racial and cultural mixing—began a few decades after [Spanish colonization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_colonization_of_the_Americas) in the early sixteenth century, when predominantly male Spanish settlers formed unions with or fathered children by [Taíno](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ta%C3%ADno_people) women and, progressively, with enslaved African women and their descendants.

The vast majority of Cubans who today identify as mestizo or mulato are not first-generation descendants of a single interracial union but bear the accumulated legacy of racial mixing across many generations—in some family lines extending across four or five centuries. Genomic studies based on [ancestry informative markers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancestry_informative_markers) estimate that the average Cuban carries approximately 72% European, 20% African, and 8% Amerindian ancestry on [autosomal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autosome) markers, while the eastern provinces show substantially higher African and indigenous contributions than the western ones.[^cite_note-marcheco2014-167][^cite_note-forteslima2018-188] Genetic studies further document a pronounced sex bias in the admixture process: male lineages are predominantly European—as evidenced by [Y chromosome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Y_chromosome) data—while female lineages carry predominantly African and Native American contributions—as detected via [mitochondrial DNA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mitochondrial_DNA)—a pattern consistent with the demographic asymmetries of the colonial [plantation economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plantation_economy).[^cite_note-marcheco2014-167] Notably, the complete absence of indigenous paternal lineages implies that virtually all Amerindian ancestry in the Cuban population is transmitted through the maternal line.[^cite_note-marcheco2014-167][^cite_note-forteslima2018-188]

The ideology of mestizaje has been central to Cuban national identity since the nineteenth century, particularly through the writings of [José Martí](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Mart%C3%AD)—especially his essay ["Nuestra América"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Our_America) (1891)—and was further reinforced after the [Cuban Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_Revolution) of 1959, when racial mixing was officially promoted as evidence of a classless and post-racial society. Critics have identified this framing as a mechanism for suppressing, rather than resolving, persistent racial inequalities.[^cite_note-A_Nation_for_All:_Race,_Inequality-189][^cite_note-190]

#### Mulato

In Cuba, *mulato* (feminine *mulata*; most likely derived from [Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin) *mulus*, meaning 'mule', evoking the colonial metaphor of racial hybridity, though some etymologists have proposed an alternative derivation from Arabic *muwallad*) refers broadly to a person of mixed African and European ancestry. The term was originally applied in colonial legal and ecclesiastical records to the first-generation offspring of one African and one European parent, but broadened in popular Cuban usage to encompass any person displaying a [phenotypic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phenotype) mixture of African and European traits, regardless of generational distance.



Cuban contemporary dancers in Havana, January 2014. Photo: Marjorie Kaufman.

Unlike the [one-drop rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/One-drop_rule) predominant in the United States—entrenched as social practice in the decades following the [American Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Civil_War) but legally formalized only in the early twentieth century, when states such as [Tennessee](30395.md) (1910) and [Virginia](32432.md) (1924) codified it into law[^cite_note-191]—[Spanish colonial law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Derecho_indiano?action=edit\&redlink=1) recognized a spectrum of racial gradations. Mechanisms such as the [gracias al sacar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gracias_al_sacar?action=edit\&redlink=1)—a royal dispensation formalized by the [Spanish Crown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Crown) in 1795, onerous and in practice accessible mainly to persons of some economic standing—allowed individuals of mixed ancestry to petition for official reclassification as Spanish, thereby conferring on the category *mulato* a distinct identity in both legal and social terms rather than subsuming it within a binary racial framework.[^cite_note-192]

The figure of the *mulata* took on powerful national and aesthetic symbolic resonance in Cuban culture from the nineteenth century onward, idealized as the embodiment of Cuban racial synthesis in [literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_literature), [music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Cuba), and the visual arts. This trope has nonetheless been widely criticized by scholars for sexualizing and objectifying [Afro-Cuban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afro-Cubans) women and for aestheticizing racial mixing while leaving intact the underlying hierarchies of anti-Black discrimination.[^cite_note-193] Admixture studies conducted on [Havana](49719.md)-based samples of self-identified mulatos have found European ancestry proportions typically ranging between 57% and 59%, and West African ancestry proportions between 41% and 43%, though individual variation is considerable; these figures should not be taken as representative of Cuba as a whole, given the marked genetic gradient between the country's western and eastern provinces.[^cite_note-marcheco2014-167][^cite_note-forteslima2018-188]

#### Jabao

*Jabao* (also spelled *javao*; feminine *jabá* or *javá*) is a colloquial Cuban racial classification term designating a person with light or near-white skin but whose African ancestry remains visually perceptible through other [phenotypic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phenotype) markers—most characteristically, very tightly coiled or curled hair (referred to in Cuban vernacular, at times pejoratively, as *pelo malo*)—as well as facial features associated with African descent. The term is used throughout the Hispanic Caribbean, including [Puerto Rico](23041.md) and the [Dominican Republic](8060.md).[^cite_note-Ortiz_1924-194]

The variant *jabao capirro* designates, in Cuban colloquial usage, a *jabao* who additionally has blond or reddish hair; the use of this compound expression is documented in Cuban lexicographic and ethnographic sources,[^cite_note-Ortiz_1924-194] though it is considerably less widely attested than the simple term. The category illustrates the broader Cuban system of racial classification by [phenotype](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phenotype) rather than by documented ancestry; popular expressions such as *los jabao no tienen raza* ('the jabao have no race') reflect the liminal and ambiguous status of the *jabao* within Cuban racial taxonomy.[^cite_note-A_Nation_for_All:_Race,_Inequality-189]

### Cubans of East Asian descent

Cuba's official censuses classified residents of East Asian descent under the [racial category](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racial_classification) of "yellow" from at least 1899 through 1981—a designation widely criticized in scholarship as reductive and inadequate.[^cite_note-delafuente2001-195] The category was abolished in the 2002 national census, which reduced Cuba's official racial classification to three categories: white (*blanco*), black (*negro*), and mixed-race (*mestizo*).[^cite_note-196] External demographic estimates place Cubans of Asian descent at roughly 1% of the total population, the large majority of Chinese origin.

#### Chinese Cubans

Main article: [Chinese Cubans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Cubans)

Chinese immigration to Cuba began in 1847, when [colonial planters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_plantation?action=edit\&redlink=1) began importing [contract laborers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coolie) to offset a growing labor shortage as international [abolitionism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abolitionism) curtailed the [transatlantic slave trade](49005.md).[^cite_note-lopez2013-197] Between 1847 and 1874, an estimated 141,000 to 150,000 men—recruited largely through coercive or fraudulent conditions from [Guangdong](65109.md)—were shipped to Cuba under eight-year [indenture contracts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indentured_labor); approximately 125,000 survived the voyage.[^cite_note-yun2008-198][^cite_note-hudehart1993-199] Because the migrant pool was almost exclusively male—women reportedly constituting under 1% of arrivals—intermarriage with [Afro-Cuban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afro-Cubans) and [white Cuban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Cubans) women was common, producing a substantial mixed-descent population over subsequent generations.[^cite_note-garcia2009-200][^cite_note-yun2008-198]

Contemporaries and later historians have broadly characterized this [coolie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coolie) indenture regime as functionally indistinguishable from [slavery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavery), observing that Chinese workers toiled alongside enslaved Africans under comparable conditions of coercion, violence, and juridical unfreedom.[^cite_note-yun2008-198][^cite_note-hudehart1993-199][^cite_note-lopez2013-197]

After completing their contracts, many former coolies settled permanently in Cuba, establishing small businesses and [mutual-aid associations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Huiguan). [Havana's *Barrio Chino*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Havana_Chinatown) grew into the largest [Chinatown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinatown) in Latin America, with the ethnic Chinese population in Cuba peaking at approximately 40,000 in the early decades of the twentieth century.[^cite_note-garcia2009-200][^cite_note-lopez2013-197] Chinese Cubans participated significantly in both the [Ten Years' War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ten_Years'_War) (1868–1878) and the [Cuban War of Independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_War_of_Independence) (1895–1898). Their military role is commemorated by the *Monumento al Soldado Chino* in [Vedado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vedado), [Havana](49719.md)—an eight-metre granite column erected in 1931—whose bronze plaque bears the inscription: *No hubo un chino cubano desertor. No hubo un chino cubano traidor* ("There was no Chinese Cuban (who was a) deserter. There was no Chinese Cuban (who was a) traitor").[^cite_note-garcia2009-200][^cite_note-yun2008-198]

The [Cuban Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_Revolution) of 1959 effectively dismantled the community. [Nationalization of private enterprise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nationalizations_in_Cuba?action=edit\&redlink=1) under [Fidel Castro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fidel_Castro) drove out most Chinese Cubans—predominantly small-scale merchants and restaurateurs—chiefly to Miami and elsewhere in Latin America.[^cite_note-lopez2013-197][^cite_note-garcia2009-200] Only an estimated few hundred ethnic Chinese remain on the island today; the *Barrio Chino* has been widely characterized as a Chinatown without Chinese.[^cite_note-garcia2009-200]

#### Other East Asian communities

#### Filipino Cubans

Main article: [Filipino Cubans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filipino_Cubans)

Cubans of [Filipino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filipino_people) descent represent one of the island's oldest Asian communities, with a presence documented from the sixteenth century. Filipino workers and sailors arrived via the [Manila–Acapulco galleon trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila_galleon) (1565–1815) and settled primarily in the western tobacco-producing region that colonial authorities designated *Nueva Filipinas* ("New Philippines"), today [Pinar del Río](460401.md).[^cite_note-201] Over subsequent centuries, this community was largely absorbed into the general Cuban population through intermarriage, leaving no ethnically distinct Filipino community today.

#### Japanese Cubans

Main article: [Japanese Cubans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japanese_Cubans)

The [Japanese community](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japanese_Cubans) in Cuba is small. The first significant groups arrived via Mexico between 1910 and 1916, many fleeing the violence of the [Mexican Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexican_Revolution).[^cite_note-masterson2004-202] Following the [attack on Pearl Harbor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attack_on_Pearl_Harbor), the [Batista](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fulgencio_Batista) government declared war on Japan on 9 December 1941; on 12 December, all Japanese residents in Cuba were formally designated [enemy aliens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enemy_alien). Most were subsequently arrested and [deported to the United States for wartime internment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japanese_Latin_Americans?action=edit\&redlink=1).[^cite_note-masterson2004-202]

#### Korean Cubans

Main article: [Koreans in Cuba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koreans_in_Cuba)

[Cuban Koreans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koreans_in_Cuba) descend from approximately 288 workers who relocated from the [Yucatán Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yucat%C3%A1n_Peninsula), Mexico, to Cuba in May 1921. Their ancestors had been among 1,033 Koreans who emigrated to the [Yucatán](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yucat%C3%A1n) in 1905 under similarly coercive contract-labor arrangements, originally destined for [henequen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henequen) plantations.[^cite_note-203] Their descendants—estimated at 800—are concentrated principally in [Havana](49719.md), [Matanzas](1386915.md), and other areas of the island.[^cite_note-204]

### Cubans of Arab descent

Cuba's [Arab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_people) community descends primarily from [Levantine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levant) emigrants – mainly from present-day [Lebanon](17771.md), [Syria](7515849.md), and [Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestine_\(region\)) — who began arriving in the 1870s, fleeing [Ottoman conscription](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_service_in_the_Ottoman_Empire?action=edit\&redlink=1), political instability, famine, and sectarian violence.[^cite_note-Fiddian2016-205][^cite_note-Khater2001-206] Because they traveled on [Ottoman](22278.md) imperial passports, Cuban authorities labeled them *turcos* ("Turks"), a [misnomer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Misnomer) that persisted for decades.[^cite_note-KlichLesser1998-207][^cite_note-Fiddian2016-205] The main immigration wave ran from the 1890s through the 1930s, peaking around [World War I](4764461.md) and its aftermath; by the 1930s, the Arab community in Cuba numbered approximately 34,000.

Immigrants were predominantly [Arab Christians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Christians) — [Maronite](66391078.md), [Greek Orthodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Orthodox_Church_of_Antioch), and other [Eastern rite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Christianity) Christians – with a smaller [Muslim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim) minority.[^cite_note-Fiddian2016-205][^cite_note-Logrono2015-208] Most settled in [Havana](49719.md), particularly in the Barrio Monte district, following the typical [Levantine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levantine_people) immigrant trajectory in Latin America: beginning as itinerant textile and goods [peddlers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peddler) before accumulating sufficient capital to open fixed commercial establishments.[^cite_note-HouraniShehadi1992-209][^cite_note-KlichLesser1998-207] The community established formal institutions, including the Syrian Society of Havana (1918), the Palestinian Society (1919), and the Lebanese Society of Havana (1920).[^cite_note-Fiddian2016-205] The [Arab Union of Cuba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Union_of_Cuba) (*Unión Árabe de Cuba*, UAC), reconstituted in 1979, remains the community's principal institutional anchor.

The [1959 Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_Revolution) dismantled the community's economic base through the [nationalization of private commerce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_nationalization?action=edit\&redlink=1), prompting significant emigration, though a substantial portion of the community remained on the island.[^cite_note-Fiddian2016-205] Cuba was the only Latin American state to vote against [United Nations General Assembly Resolution 181](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_General_Assembly_Resolution_181) — the 1947 [Palestine partition plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Partition_Plan_for_Palestine) — a position attributed to both Cuba's [anti-colonial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-imperialism) principles and the political influence of its Arab community.[^cite_note-UNRES181-210][^cite_note-KlichLesser1998-207]

### Cuban Jews

The Cuban Jewish community is one of the oldest and most historically layered in the [Caribbean](18956035.md), with roots traceable to the earliest years of [Spanish colonization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_colonization_of_the_Americas). [Luis de Torres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luis_de_Torres)—a [converso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Converso) who served as [Columbus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christopher_Columbus)'s interpreter of Arabic and [Hebrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_language)—figures in the historical record as one of the first Europeans to set foot on Cuban soil in 1492.[^cite_note-EJ2007-211][^cite_note-Levine1993-212] A modern, organized, and sustained community, however, only emerged in the twentieth century, through three distinct waves of immigration: [Ashkenazi Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashkenazi_Jews) who arrived alongside the expansion of US commercial interests after 1898 and who founded the United Hebrew Congregation in 1906;[^cite_note-Levine1993-212] [Sephardic Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sephardic_Jews) from the [Ottoman Empire](22278.md), primarily from [Turkey](11125639.md) and the [Levant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levant), who accounted for approximately 90 percent of the island's roughly 1,000 Jews by 1918;[^cite_note-Levine1993-212][^cite_note-Bejarano1996-213] and Eastern European Ashkenazi Jews who arrived from the 1920s onward, most of them [Yiddish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yiddish_language)-speaking and stranded in Cuba by the quotas imposed by the [Immigration Act of 1924](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immigration_Act_of_1924).[^cite_note-Levine1993-212][^cite_note-Bejarano1996-213]

Cuba was also the setting for one of the most thoroughly documented episodes in the history of wartime [refugee](45547.md) policy. On 13 May 1939, the German ocean liner [MS St. Louis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MS_St._Louis) departed [Hamburg](13467.md) for [Havana](49719.md) carrying 937 passengers, nearly all of them Jews fleeing [Nazi Germany](21212.md). Cuban President [Federico Laredo Brú](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federico_Laredo_Br%C3%BA) annulled by executive decree all previously issued disembarkation certificates, and when the ship arrived in Havana on 27 May, only 28 passengers whose documentation was in order were allowed to disembark.[^cite_note-OgilvieMiller2006-214][^cite_note-USHMM_StLouis-215] After being similarly turned away by the United States and Canada, the ship was compelled to return to Europe; of the 907 passengers who sailed back, approximately 254 subsequently perished in the [Holocaust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holocaust).[^cite_note-OgilvieMiller2006-214][^cite_note-Thomas1974-216]

At its peak in the 1950s, the Cuban Jewish community numbered around 15,000.[^cite_note-Levine1993-212][^cite_note-Bejarano1996-213] The [Revolution of 1959](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_Revolution) and the [nationalization of private commerce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_nationalization?action=edit\&redlink=1)—a sector in which Cuban Jews were heavily concentrated—prompted between 90 and 94 percent of the community to emigrate.[^cite_note-Levine1993-212][^cite_note-Bejarano1996-213] Today approximately 1,500 remain, the majority in [Havana](49719.md), with an exceptionally high [intermarriage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interfaith_marriage) rate and no resident [rabbi](51273.md) since the revolution.[^cite_note-EJ2007-211][^cite_note-Bejarano1996-213]

## Genetics

Genetic studies characterize the Cuban population as substantially [admixed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multiracial), reflecting the island's history of European colonization, the forced migration of enslaved Africans, and the prior presence of [indigenous populations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_peoples_of_the_Caribbean).

An [autosomal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genealogical_DNA_test) admixture study of 1,019 individuals drawn from all Cuban provinces, published in 2014, estimated average genetic ancestry of 72% European, 20% African, and 8% [Native American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_peoples_of_the_Americas).[^cite_note-Cuba:_Exploring_the_History_of_Admi-217] There is substantial intra-island variation: western provinces exhibit a higher average European ancestry, while eastern provinces—most notably Guantánamo and Santiago de Cuba, where African ancestry reaches approximately 39–40%—show markedly elevated African and Native American contributions.[^cite_note-Cuba:_Exploring_the_History_of_Admi-217][^cite_note-Exploring_Cuba's_population_structu-218] These geographic patterns are consistent with historical and demographic records.

A 2018 genome-wide study of 860 individuals further identified the [Iberian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iberian_Peninsula) population as the closest proxy for the European component, [West Central and Central African](67393.md) populations for the African component, and South American and [Mesoamerican](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mesoamerica) populations for the Native American component.[^cite_note-Exploring_Cuba's_population_structu-218] Both the 2014 and 2018 studies identified a strongly sex-biased pattern in the admixture process: European ancestry is proportionally higher in paternal ([Y-chromosome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Y-chromosome_haplogroup)) lineages, while African and Native American ancestry is more strongly represented in maternal ([mtDNA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_mitochondrial_DNA_haplogroup)) lineages.[^cite_note-Genetic_origin,_admixture,_and_asym-219][^cite_note-Cuba:_Exploring_the_History_of_Admi-217] This asymmetry reflects the historical demographics of the colonial period, in which Spanish immigration consisted predominantly of men, who mixed primarily with indigenous and enslaved African women.

An early study of residents of [Pinar del Río](460401.md), published in 1995 in the context of an investigation into epidemic optic neuropathy, analyzed [mitochondrial DNA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_mitochondrial_DNA_haplogroup) haplotypes and found that approximately 50% of mtDNA lineages traced to European sources, 46% to African, and 4% to Native American origins, consistent with the demographic history and census composition of that province.[^cite_note-220]

A broader 2008 study sampling individuals from multiple Cuban provinces found a different national-level mtDNA distribution: approximately 22% of sequences were of West Eurasian origin (European and Middle Eastern), 45% of African origin, and 33% of Native American origin.[^cite_note-Genetic_origin,_admixture,_and_asym-219] The considerably lower West Eurasian fraction in the maternal lineages, compared to the autosomal estimate, reflects the sex-biased nature of Cuban colonial-era admixture. Regarding [Y-chromosome haplogroups](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_Y-chromosome_DNA_haplogroup) (paternal lineages), the same 2008 study found that approximately 79% of Cuban male sequences are of West Eurasian origin, approximately 20% of African origin, and approximately 1.5% of East Asian origin; no Native American paternal lineages were detected.[^cite_note-Genetic_origin,_admixture,_and_asym-219] Among the West Eurasian fraction, the majority of individuals belong to [haplogroup R1b](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haplogroup_R1b_\(Y-DNA\)), the predominant Western European paternal lineage. The African haplogroups detected in Cubans derive from both Western African sources (haplogroups [E1a](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haplogroup_E1a_\(Y-DNA\)), [E1b1a](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haplogroup_E1b1a_\(Y-DNA\))) and North African sources ([E1b1b-M81](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haplogroup_E1b1b_\(Y-DNA\))). The North African haplogroup E-M81, which is characteristic of [Berber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Berber_people) populations and is also elevated in the [Canary Islands](5717.md), is present in Cuban males at a frequency of approximately 6.1%.[^cite_note-Genetic_origin,_admixture,_and_asym-219] According to Fregel et al. (2009), autochthonous male (E-M81) and female (U6) haplogroup lineages of indigenous [Guanche](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guanches) derivation from the [Canary Islands](5717.md) have been detected in populations across [Iberoamerica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iberoamerica), indicating that Canary Islanders with indigenous [North African-derived](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Berber_people) ancestry participated actively in the colonization of the Americas.[^cite_note-221]

## Diaspora

See also: [Cuban immigration to the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_immigration_to_the_United_States) and [Cuban immigration to Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_immigration_to_Mexico)



Current Cuban passport.

The Cuban diaspora is one of the most sustained and well-documented migratory phenomena in the Western Hemisphere. Over more than a century and a half—from the first communities of tobacco workers and political exiles in the nineteenth century to the migration crisis unfolding since 2021—emigration has been a structural constant in Cuban history, intertwined with political upheaval, economic crises, and the character of successive governing regimes. The tobacco worker and independence-era exile communities of [Key West](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Key_West), [Tampa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tampa), and New York City were precursors to one of the most significant exoduses of the twentieth century, which in its contemporary phase has produced a demographic hemorrhage without precedent among Latin American nations.

### The nineteenth-century proto-diaspora: tobacco industry, independence, and political exile

The history of Cuban emigration as an organized, politically conscious phenomenon does not begin with the revolution of 1959 but in the nineteenth century, against the backdrop of the [independence wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_War_of_Independence) against Spanish colonial rule. From the mid-1800s, groups of Cubans—primarily tobacco industry workers and sectors of the Creole petty bourgeoisie—had established stable communities in Florida, New York, and other northeastern US cities, as well as in Mexico, Spain, and Venezuela.

The development of the tobacco industry in Florida drove the first significant concentrations of Cuban emigrants on US soil. Entrepreneur [Vicente Martinez Ybor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vicente_Martinez_Ybor) relocated his factory to the outskirts of Tampa in 1885, founding [Ybor City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ybor_City), conceived as an industrial and residential complex for tobacco workers, mostly Cubans, Spaniards, and Sicilians.[^cite_note-222] A fire in [Key West](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Key_West) in 1886 accelerated the transfer of workers to Tampa, consolidating Ybor City as the largest Cuban community in the nineteenth-century United States. Key West had itself harbored a substantial colony of Cuban cigar workers who, through the practice of the *[lector de tabaquería](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lector_\(tobacco\)?action=edit\&redlink=1)*, sustained the patriotic and independence-minded ethos of the Cuban emigrant community.

These communities also served as nodes of political agitation and fundraising for the independence movement. [José Martí](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Mart%C3%AD) visited Tampa on multiple occasions; it was at the Liceo Cubano in Ybor City that he delivered, on 26 November 1891, some of his best-known speeches—*[Con todos y para el bien de todos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Con_todos_y_para_el_bien_de_todos?action=edit\&redlink=1)* and *[Los pinos nuevos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Los_pinos_nuevos?action=edit\&redlink=1)*.[^cite_note-223] In 1892, in Tampa, he drafted the programmatic charter of the [Cuban Revolutionary Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_Revolutionary_Party). Working-class communities in Key West, Ybor City, and West Tampa contributed regular donations to the war effort, and some of their members participated in the military expeditions that landed in Cuba.

President Richard Nixon with Charles Bebe Rebozo at Key Biscayne

President Richard Nixon with his friend and confidant Charles "Bebe" Rebozo, a banker whose family emigrated to Tampa in the late nineteenth century as part of the pre-revolutionary Cuban diaspora. A close associate of Nixon for more than two decades, Rebozo exerted a discreet but considerable influence and was a central figure in his circle at [Key Biscayne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Key_Biscayne), Florida.

This first Cuban emigration was doubly characterized: as a labor migration—driven by disruptions in the tobacco and sugar markets and the political turbulence of colonial rule—and as a political diaspora organized around the independence project. The Cuban colony in New York, where Martí resided for extended periods and coordinated exile political activity, reinforced this dimension: Cuban intellectuals, journalists, and political figures articulated, through the Spanish-language press and civic organization, a Cuban national identity in exile before the independent state had come into existence.

With the conclusion of the independence war, the US military intervention of 1898, and the establishment of the Cuban Republic in 1902 under the [Platt Amendment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Platt_Amendment), many emigrants returned. Nevertheless, migratory currents between Cuba and the United States did not cease. On the eve of 1 January 1959, approximately 60,600 Cubans resided in the United States, with the largest concentration in New York.[^cite_note-224]

A distinct episode within this period involves the opponents of [Fulgencio Batista](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fulgencio_Batista)'s regime (1952–1958), whose 1952 coup disrupted constitutional order and drove politicians, intellectuals, and democratic activists into exile—many of them to Mexico, where Fidel Castro organized the *[Granma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Granma_\(boat\))* expedition. Paradoxically, some of these anti-Batista exiles became, a few years later, among the first emigrants of post-revolutionary Cuba.

### The post-revolutionary exodus (1959–1962): the "Golden Exile"



[Celia Cruz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Celia_Cruz) (Havana, 1925 – New Jersey, 2003), known as the "Queen of Salsa", photographed in 1957. A vocalist with [La Sonora Matancera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Sonora_Matancera), she left Cuba in July 1960 and was subsequently barred from returning. She became a symbol of the Cuban exile and one of the most internationally prominent Latin voices of the twentieth century.

The establishment of the revolutionary government on 1 January 1959, triggered an unprecedented wave of mass emigration. Within six months, approximately 500 exiles had arrived in Miami; by end-1960 the figure had risen to 33,000, and by October 1962—when the [Cuban Missile Crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_Missile_Crisis) severed commercial air links between the two countries—approximately 250,000 Cubans had emigrated.[^cite_note-225]

This first wave was designated the "Golden Exile", a reference to its predominantly privileged sociological composition. The first to leave were collaborators of the Batista regime; they were followed by a larger contingent of upper- and upper-middle-class Cubans: business owners, professionals, merchants, executives, and families with economic ties to US capital. The policies that drove this initial exodus included the forced redistribution of private property, the nationalization of religious and private schools, the suppression of civil associations, and the sweeping nationalizations of enterprises and industries from mid-1960 onward. The government's alignment with the Soviet bloc, unambiguous throughout 1960 and officially proclaimed in 1961, reinforced the conviction among emigrants that return was foreclosed. The Cuban government, which from the outset characterized emigrants as *gusanos* ("worms"), promoted an image of a class-privileged diaspora that distorted the political dimensions of the exile.

Among the most extensively documented episodes of this period is [Operation Peter Pan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Peter_Pan), organized between 1960 and 1962 with the participation of the Cuban Catholic Church, the Catholic Church in Miami, and the US government. Concerned that the state would assume increasing control over the upbringing of minors, thousands of families sent their children abroad unaccompanied. Between December 1960 and October 1962, more than 14,000 Cuban children traveled alone to the United States and were placed with foster families or religious institutions in Florida and other states.[^cite_note-226][^cite_note-227]



[Jorge Mas Canosa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jorge_Mas_Canosa) with President [Bill Clinton](3356.md). Founder of the [Cuban American National Foundation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_American_National_Foundation) (CANF) in 1981, Mas Canosa was the most influential political figure of the Cuban exile and the principal organizer of Cuban-American lobby pressure on Washington foreign policy during the Reagan, Bush, and Clinton administrations.

The impact of this first wave on Miami was transformative. The Kennedy administration created the [Cuban Refugee Program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_Refugee_Program?action=edit\&redlink=1) in 1961.[^cite_note-228] The [Cuban Adjustment Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_Adjustment_Act), enacted in 1966 and signed by President Johnson, allowed any Cuban national present in the United States for at least one year to apply for lawful permanent residence, regardless of manner of entry.[^cite_note-229]

### The Freedom Flights (1965–1973)

The period between the October 1962 missile crisis and the opening of the port of [Camarioca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camarioca) in September 1965 was marked by a reduction in large-scale migration, though clandestine emigration continued. In September 1965, the Cuban government opened Camarioca and announced that Cubans abroad could collect relatives by sea. Hundreds of vessels set out from Florida and approximately 5,000 Cubans emigrated before the port was closed in October. This episode served as the prelude to a bilateral agreement that opened a regulated emigration channel: the [Freedom Flights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_Airlift?action=edit\&redlink=1).

Between December 1965 and April 1973, two daily flights connected Varadero with Miami. Through this program, approximately 260,600 Cubans emigrated to the United States.[^cite_note-230][^cite_note-231] The demographic profile of this wave already differed from the Golden Exile: middle and working classes were more prominently represented, though the Cuban government imposed restrictions to retain men of working age and professionals in sectors deemed strategic.

During these years, Miami consolidated its role as the capital of the Cuban exile. [Calle Ocho](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calle_Ocho) (Southwest 8th Street) became the commercial and cultural axis of [Little Havana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Little_Havana), and the Cuban-American community developed growing political influence at the local, state, and ultimately federal levels.

The "Cuban success" narrative was subject to critical revision from the 1970s onward. The image of a homogeneously white, conservative, and economically successful diaspora obscured the experiences of Black and mixed-race Cubans, the working classes, and those who faced discrimination within the exile community itself. Afro-Cubans constituted only approximately 3% of arrivals between 1959 and 1973—a disproportion reflecting both the composition of the early cohorts and the effects of official revolutionary rhetoric, which cast emigration as a class betrayal with explicit racial connotations.[^cite_note-232]

### The Mariel exodus (1980)

The period from 1973 to 1980 saw a slowdown in mass migration flows.[^cite_note-233] Within Cuba, mounting social tension had accumulated: the failure of the 1970 ten-million-ton sugar harvest had eroded the regime's legitimacy, and the cultural repression symbolized by the [Padilla affair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Padilla_affair) of 1971—when poet [Heberto Padilla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heberto_Padilla) was detained and coerced into a public self-criticism—had alienated sectors of the intelligentsia both inside and outside the island.[^cite_note-234]

Demonstrators in Havana in support of the Cuban government during the Mariel boatlift

Supporters of the Cuban socialist government demonstrating against those seeking to leave the country during the [Mariel boatlift](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mariel_boatlift), 1980.

In March and April 1980, groups of Cubans forced entry into the Peruvian and Venezuelan embassies in Havana seeking asylum. The government withdrew the guard from the Peruvian embassy and announced that any Cuban wishing to leave could do so without hindrance. Within days, more than 10,000 people had gathered on the embassy grounds.[^cite_note-235][^cite_note-236] The government then opened the port of Mariel to vessels willing to collect emigrants.

Cuban refugees on a boat during the Mariel boatlift

Cuban refugees arriving in Florida during the [Mariel boatlift](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mariel_boatlift), 1980.

Between 15 April – 31 October 1980, approximately 125,000 Cubans crossed the Florida Strait in the [Mariel boatlift](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mariel_boatlift). The *marielitos* came in far larger proportions from the working classes: between 15 and 40% were Afro-Cuban, compared with approximately 3% in previous waves. Many had grown up in post-1959 Cuba with no direct experience of the previous regime, and their decision to emigrate stemmed from frustration at limited social mobility and individual freedom rather than from the loss of a prior social position. The Cuban government deliberately included among the emigrants persons with criminal records and psychiatric patients. While precise figures remain subject to historiographical debate, as many as 20,000 arrivals had some form of criminal record; in many cases the relevant offenses were criminalized exclusively under Cuban law under provisions such as *peligrosidad predelictiva* ("pre-criminal dangerousness"), which penalized conduct including homosexuality and political dissidence.

Economist [David Card](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Card) published a landmark 1990 study on the impact of the boatlift on Miami's labor market—one of the most cited natural experiments in immigration economics—concluding that the sudden arrival of 125,000 workers did not significantly depress native workers' wages.[^cite_note-237] The methodological debate generated by that study remains active in the academic literature.[^cite_note-238]

### The Special Period and emigration in the 1980s and 1990s

The collapse of the Soviet bloc between 1989 and 1991 had severe consequences for the Cuban economy, which had depended on Soviet subsidies and preferential trade. In 1990, the Cuban government proclaimed the [Special Period in Time of Peace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_Period_in_Time_of_Peace), the official designation for an economic crisis entailing sharp GDP contractions, food shortages, and the collapse of transportation and basic services.[^cite_note-239] Between 1990 and July 1994, approximately 61,200 Cubans were admitted to the United States through regular channels, and some 13,600 more arrived in improvised watercraft. Clandestine maritime emigration—the [balseros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balsero), who crossed the ninety miles of the Florida Strait on tire tubes, wooden rafts, and other improvised floating objects—intensified into a permanent, high-mortality flow.

### The 1994 balsero crisis

Cuban-American demonstrators in Washington D.C. in October 1994

Cuban-American demonstrators in Washington, D.C., in October 1994, protesting against President [Bill Clinton](3356.md)'s policy of intercepting [balseros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1994_Cuban_rafter_crisis) and transferring them to [Naval Station Guantanamo Bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naval_Station_Guantanamo_Bay) rather than receiving them as refugees.

The summer of 1994 triggered the most severe migration crisis since Mariel. On 5 August, a crowd gathered on Havana's Malecón promenade in a spontaneous outburst of popular discontent—the [Maleconazo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maleconazo)—the most significant public anti-government protest in decades.[^cite_note-240] On 13 August, Castro announced that the government would not prevent departures by sea. The [1994 Cuban rafter crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1994_Cuban_rafter_crisis) consisted of the exodus of more than 35,000 Cubans over a five-week period beginning 13 August 1994.[^cite_note-241]

The Clinton administration ordered on 19 August that rafters be intercepted and transferred to camps at [Naval Station Guantanamo Bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naval_Station_Guantanamo_Bay) and in Panama rather than admitted to the continental United States, thereby abandoning the unconditional reception policy that had been in effect until then.[^cite_note-242] In September 1994 and May 1995, the two governments concluded agreements establishing a US commitment to grant a minimum of 20,000 annual visas to Cuban nationals, and the [wet foot/dry foot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wet_feet,_dry_feet) policy: Cubans intercepted at sea would be returned, while those who managed to reach US soil could apply for residency.[^cite_note-243][^cite_note-244] This policy, in effect until January 2017, generated incentives for increasingly risky crossing methods at a human cost that no official statistics could precisely document.



Cuban migrants disembarking from the [USS *Vicksburg*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USS_Vicksburg_\(CG-69\)) at the U.S. Naval Base at Guantanamo Bay, 1994. They were among the first Cubans to arrive aboard a U.S. naval vessel during [Operation Able Vigil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Able_Vigil?action=edit\&redlink=1).

### Diaspora in the 1990s and 2000s

The period following the balsero crisis was one of sustained but non-mass emigration, channeled in part through the 1994 visa framework and in part through irregular overland migration via Central America and Mexico. The Cuban-American community completed a political transformation begun in the 1970s: from an initial refugee community with limited electoral participation to one with structural electoral weight in Florida, overwhelmingly conservative in orientation. The Cuban-American community was a decisive factor in the outcome of the 2000 presidential election in that state.[^cite_note-245]

Through the 2000s, the composition and attitudes of the diaspora began to shift. More recent arrivals came in larger proportions from working-class backgrounds, were more racially diverse, and had lived their entire lives under the post-1959 regime. Tensions between the "historic exile"—with its emphasis on property restitution, the embargo, and non-negotiation with the regime—and younger generations more inclined toward family contact and remittances began to erode the political unanimity that had long characterized Miami's exile community.

### Sustained emigration in the 2000s and 2010s

Through the 2000s and 2010s, Cuban emigration remained quantitatively significant, with overland routes through Central America and Mexico gaining in importance. One contributing factor was that Ecuador did not require a Cuban visa until 2015, allowing Cubans to fly there and begin the overland journey northward.

Cuban refugees aboard the Carnival Liberty cruise ship after being rescued at sea, August 2014

Cuban refugees rescued at sea by the cruise ship *Carnival Liberty* in August 2014. Improvised vessel crossings remained a common mode of escape decades after the 1980 [Mariel boatlift](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mariel_boatlift).

Between 2014 and 2016, thousands of Cubans were stranded in Costa Rica after Nicaragua closed its border in November 2015. Most were eventually authorized to transit toward the Mexican border through a regional agreement among the governments of Costa Rica, El Salvador, Guatemala, Honduras, Belize, Mexico, and Panama, with logistical coordination by the [International Organization for Migration](36893.md) (IOM).[^cite_note-246]

President Obama's 17 December 2014, announcement of the normalization of diplomatic relations with Cuba[^cite_note-247] had paradoxical effects on migration. In anticipation of the expected repeal of wet foot/dry foot—which occurred on 12 January 2017—flows accelerated sharply in 2015 and 2016. In fiscal year 2016, the United States recorded more than 56,000 Cuban arrivals, the highest figure since Mariel.[^cite_note-248] The revocation of wet foot/dry foot in January 2017 meant that, for the first time since the 1960s, Cubans intercepted at sea or at the land border without documentation were processed under ordinary asylum law, without automatic access to permanent residency.[^cite_note-249][^cite_note-250]

### The 2021–2026 migration crisis: the largest exodus in Cuban history

#### Background and triggering factors

The migration process that intensified from 2021 onward is unprecedented in the history of Cuban emigration for its volume, speed, and demographic impact on the island. Its origins lie in the convergence of structural and circumstantial factors.

Economically, the [COVID-19 pandemic](62750956.md) compounded an economy already deteriorating since the mid-2010s, itself a product of Venezuela's economic collapse—which had been the island's principal supplier of subsidized oil—the tightening of US sanctions under the Trump administration from 2017, and the structural inability of the state economic model to finance imports through its own exports.[^cite_note-251][^cite_note-252] Tourism collapsed with the pandemic.[^cite_note-253] Shortages of food, medicine, and fuel reached levels not seen since the 1990s; power outages extending up to sixteen to twenty hours daily in some provinces became recurrent. Cuba's healthcare system showed marked deterioration during the pandemic due to shortages of drugs, basic supplies, equipment, and personnel.[^cite_note-254]

Politically, the [protests of July 11, 2021](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Cuban_protests)—known as *11J*—marked an inflection point: the largest public demonstrations against the regime since the establishment of the one-party republic, with crowds taking to the streets across dozens of cities and towns under slogans including "[Patria y Vida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patria_y_Vida)!", "¡Libertad!", and "¡Se acabó!".[^cite_note-255] The government's response included internet blackouts,[^cite_note-256] mass detentions, prison sentences of up to thirty years imposed on protesters who had committed no acts of violence, and the deployment of paramilitary groups.[^cite_note-257][^cite_note-258] The repression of 11J, combined with continuing material deterioration, drove an emigration wave reaching segments of the population that had until then remained on the island.

#### Scale of the exodus and demographic impact

According to data from [U.S. Customs and Border Protection](757040.md) (CBP) and the [United States Department of Homeland Security](58236.md), between October 2021 and mid-2024 more than 860,000 Cubans arrived in the United States, the majority via the land border with Mexico.[^cite_note-259] December 2022 saw the peak of the exodus, with 44,064 Cuban arrivals in a single month—a figure equivalent to the entire total for 2012. In calendar year 2022 alone, 313,488 Cubans arrived in the United States, representing approximately 3% of Cuba's population for that year; this single-year total surpassed the cumulative figures of the Golden Exile (354,963 between 1959 and 1962), the Freedom Flights (333,457 between 1965 and 1973), and every preceding Cuban migration episode. Total emigration toward all destinations in 2022 reached 369,393.

The demographic impact was confirmed by Cuban authorities. During the July 2024 session of the [National Assembly of People's Power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Assembly_of_People's_Power), Juan Carlos Alfonso Fraga, deputy director of the [National Office of Statistics and Information](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oficina_Nacional_de_Estad%C3%ADsticas_e_Informaci%C3%B3n?action=edit\&redlink=1) (ONEI), reported that Cuba's effective population had fallen to 10,055,968 inhabitants at the close of 2023—a decline of 10.1% from 2020, returning the country to demographic levels last recorded in the mid-1980s.[^cite_note-260][^cite_note-261] According to ONEI figures, Cuba's population fell from 11,181,595 on 31 December 2021, to 10,055,968 at the close of 2023—a reduction of more than one million persons in two years. The emigration of 1,011,269 persons was identified as the primary driver of this decline, followed by 405,512 deaths against only 284,892 births during the same period.[^cite_note-reuters2024-262] These estimates are based on the concept of "effective population", counting persons who accumulated at least 180 days of residence in Cuba during the preceding twelve months—a methodology adopted to more accurately reflect the impact of mass emigration since 2021.[^cite_note-263]

The official figures have been subject to methodological scrutiny by independent researchers. Demographer Juan Carlos Albizu-Campos estimated, drawing on migration data, vital records, and demographic statistics, that Cuba's effective resident population may have fallen to approximately 8.62 million at the close of 2023—a reduction of close to 18% between 2022 and 2023.[^cite_note-264][^cite_note-reuters2024-262] In a subsequent update, Albizu-Campos estimated the effective resident population may have fallen to around eight million inhabitants at end-2024, representing a cumulative decline of approximately 24% from 2020.[^cite_note-265][^cite_note-lemonde2025-266] ONEI reported an effective population of 9,748,007 as of 31 December 2024, a decline of 307,961 from the previous year at a rate of −30.6 per thousand inhabitants.[^cite_note-267][^cite_note-lemonde2025-266]

Of the more than one million persons who emigrated between 2022 and 2023, approximately 800,000 were aged 15 to 59—that is, of working age—compounding the impact on the island's economic structure and reducing the active labor force base. This pattern has been identified by ONEI and by independent demographic analyses as a key factor in the deterioration of Cuba's age structure.[^cite_note-reuters2024-262] The emigration of women of reproductive age further depresses birth rates in a context of persistently sub-replacement fertility, below replacement level since the late 1970s. Albizu-Campos has noted that the magnitude of Cuba's population decline since 2020 is structurally comparable, in demographic terms, to processes historically associated with armed conflict, given the combination of mass emigration, low fertility, and accelerated ageing.[^cite_note-reuters2024-262][^cite_note-268] Multiple independent demographic analyses concur that the combination of sustained emigration, low fertility, and rapid ageing constitutes a structural crisis with long-term effects on Cuba's economy and the sustainability of its social system.[^cite_note-lemonde2025-266][^cite_note-269]

#### The Central American route

The principal artery of the 2022–2023 exodus was the Central American route, made viable by Nicaragua's decision to waive the visa requirement for Cuban nationals on 22 November 2021.[^cite_note-270] This allowed Cubans to fly directly to Managua and from there undertake the overland journey through Honduras, Guatemala, Mexico, and ultimately the US border. Between fiscal years 2022 and 2024, more than 514,000 Cubans crossed the [Darién Gap](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dari%C3%A9n_Gap) jungle—the border passage between Colombia and Panama—and Mexican territory to reach the US southern border.

The CHNV (Cuba, Haiti, Nicaragua, Venezuela) humanitarian parole program, launched by the Biden administration in January 2023, permitted the legal entry of up to 30,000 persons per month from those four countries. By October 2024, more than 110,000 Cubans had used this channel.[^cite_note-271] The Trump administration, upon taking office in January 2025, suspended the program, cancelled previously granted paroles, and announced accelerated deportation policies, reducing flows toward the United States and redirecting them toward other destinations.[^cite_note-272]

#### Diversification of migration destinations

A distinctive feature of the post-2021 exodus is the diversification of destinations. Where the United States had for decades been the primary destination of Cuban emigration, the contemporary crisis has redistributed the diaspora across a range of countries without precedent in Cuban migration history.

##### Spain

Spain has become the second most important destination, favored by cultural and linguistic proximity, an already established Cuban community, and the application of the [Democratic Memory Law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Memory_Law) of 2022, which permitted descendants of emigrants from the Spanish Civil War and the Franco era to apply for Spanish nationality.[^cite_note-273] Given that tens of thousands of Cuban families have direct Spanish ancestry, many Cubans were able to avail themselves of this legislation to obtain EU passports. According to the Spanish [National Statistics Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Instituto_Nacional_de_Estad%C3%ADstica_\(Spain\)) (INE), as of 1 January 2023, 198,639 persons born in Cuba were residing in Spain.[^cite_note-274] Cubans were the nationality that most benefited from the Democratic Memory Law: by end-2023 more than 226,000 applications had been submitted, with Havana as the leading consulate of origin. In 2023 at least 28,900 Cubans emigrated to Spain, and 33,900 in 2024, for an approximate two-year total of 62,800. The Cuban community resident in Spain exceeds 257,000 individuals by conservative estimates.

##### Brazil

Main article: [Cuban immigration to Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_immigration_to_Brazil)

Brazil has become a significant destination for Cuban migration in South America, particularly within recent flows of international protection applications and immigration regularization. According to data from the Observatory of International Migration (OBMigra), linked to Brazil's National Migration Registry System, the number of Cuban nationals with migration registration in Brazil increased from 28,374 during 2011–2020 to 103,427 during 2021–2025.[^cite_note-275] Brazil recorded 68,159 asylum applications in 2024, of which 22,288 were from Cuban nationals, making them the second largest applicant group behind Venezuelans.[^cite_note-276] UNHCR and media reports based on official data indicate that during certain months of 2025 Cubans led asylum application numbers in Brazil, surpassing Venezuelans, reflecting a shift in the composition of regional migratory flows.[^cite_note-277] Most Cuban migrants enter through the northern border in the state of Roraima, from where a significant proportion relocate to southern and southeastern regions, particularly São Paulo.[^cite_note-278]

##### Uruguay, Costa Rica, and Mexico

Uruguay recorded 7,413 Cuban asylum applications in 2024 and a further 1,700 in the first quarter of 2025.[^cite_note-279] The [IOM](36893.md) notes that Costa Rica has also become a significant settlement destination: a survey conducted among Cubans present in the country in January and February 2026 found that 94% intended to remain, citing institutional stability and access to international protection.[^cite_note-280] Mexico remains statistically a transit country for most migrants, though a growing number have settled there; in 2024 it recorded 17,884 Cuban asylum applications.[^cite_note-281]

##### The Balkans

The "Balkan route" was used by Cuban migrants primarily between 2021 and 2023 as part of irregular flows toward the European Union. Investigations coordinated by Europol and Interpol documented people-smuggling networks that exploited the visa-free regime then in effect between Cuba and Serbia to transport migrants by air to Belgrade, from where they continued their irregular transit through the Western Balkans toward EU member states.[^cite_note-europol2023-282][^cite_note-283] Criminal networks charged approximately €9,000 per person for the full journey, including transport, accommodation, and falsified documentation; an estimated 5,000 Cuban nationals were introduced irregularly into the EU through this corridor.[^cite_note-europol2023-282] Europol's analysis indicates that migrants flew from Cuba to Serbia and were subsequently transported by land through North Macedonia and Greece, with Spain as the primary final destination. In 2023, Serbia introduced visa restrictions for Cuban nationals as part of its progressive alignment with EU migration policy and in response to the use of its territory as a transit point toward the Schengen area.[^cite_note-284]

## Culture

Main article: [Culture of Cuba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Cuba)

Cuban culture is the result of a historical process of [transculturation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transculturation)—a term coined by ethnologist and sociologist [Fernando Ortiz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fernando_Ortiz_Fern%C3%A1ndez) in his work *[Cuban Counterpoint: Tobacco and Sugar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_Counterpoint:_Tobacco_and_Sugar?action=edit\&redlink=1)* (1940)—[^cite_note-ortiz1940-285] in which European traditions, primarily Spanish in origin, the traditions of various sub-Saharan African ethnic groups, and the indigenous traditions of the [Taíno](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ta%C3%ADno) and [Ciboney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ciboney) peoples converged over the course of five centuries. This process, which unfolded under conditions of colonialism, slavery, and successive waves of migration, gave rise to a distinct cultural identity of universal reach, whose most visible expressions are music, dance, literature, architecture, the visual arts, and cuisine. Cuba has exerted an influence on twentieth-century international popular culture that is notable and disproportionate to its size, above all through music and film.[^cite_note-286]

### Historical roots and cultural composition

The indigenous presence in Cuba, primarily that of the [Taíno](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ta%C3%ADno) and [Ciboney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ciboney) peoples, underwent a near-total demographic collapse in the first decades of Spanish colonization in the sixteenth century.[^cite_note-287] Their legacy, however, persists in the vocabulary—words such as *tobacco*, *[hurricane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hurricane)*, *maize*, *[yuca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yuca)*, *[canoe](99960.md)*, and *[hammock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hammock)* are of [Arawakan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arawakan_languages) origin—[^cite_note-288] as well as in agriculture and cuisine. Spanish colonization, which began with the founding of [Baracoa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baracoa) in 1511, laid the foundations of the Castilian language, ecclesiastical institutions, monumental architecture, and the literary forms that would shape the island's high culture for centuries.[^cite_note-289]

The trade in enslaved Africans, which intensified from the seventeenth century onward and reached its height between the late eighteenth and mid-nineteenth centuries—when Cuba became the world's leading sugar producer—[^cite_note-290] brought to the island populations from [Yoruba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yoruba_people) (known in Cuba as [Lucumí](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lucum%C3%AD_people)), [Bantu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bantu_peoples) (Kongo), [Fon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fon_people) ([Arará](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arar%C3%A1)), and [Efik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Efik_people)–[Ibibio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibibio_people) ([Abakuá](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abaku%C3%A1)) ethnic groups,[^cite_note-291][^cite_note-292] whose religious, musical, linguistic, and culinary systems merged with European ones to produce the cultural forms most characteristic of Cuban identity. Transculturation operated under the structural violence of slavery, yet gave rise to one of the most complex cultural syntheses in the Americas.[^cite_note-ortiz1940-285][^cite_note-293]

Subsequent waves of migration added further layers: [Canarian](5717.md) settlers, whose imprint on folk customs and rural tradition remains perceptible in rural areas;[^cite_note-naranjo2009-294] Haitian and Jamaican immigrants who arrived in the [Oriente](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oriente_Province) region from the late eighteenth century onward, bringing their own musical traditions;[^cite_note-295] Chinese contract workers who arrived between 1847 and 1874, leaving a lasting mark on cuisine;[^cite_note-296] and Catalan, Asturian, Galician, and Basque communities established primarily within the urban commercial bourgeoisie.[^cite_note-naranjo2009-294] All these elements accumulated unevenly according to region, social class, and historical period, producing the internal heterogeneity that characterizes Cuban culture.

#### Tobacco culture



Harvesting tobacco leaves on a *vega* (tobacco plantation) in [Vuelta Abajo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vuelta_Abajo), [Pinar del Río](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pinar_del_R%C3%ADo).

Tobacco is one of the pillars of Cuban cultural identity and the agricultural product through which Cuba has acquired the greatest international renown.[^cite_note-Ortiz2002-297] Cultivated and consumed by the [Taíno](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ta%C3%ADno_people) before the arrival of Europeans, it was introduced to the European continent from Cuba by Spanish colonizers in the sixteenth century.[^cite_note-Ortiz2002-297] The [Vuelta Abajo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vuelta_Abajo) region, in the present-day [Pinar del Río Province](460401.md), particularly the municipalities of [San Juan y Martínez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Juan_y_Mart%C3%ADnez), [San Luis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Luis,_Pinar_del_R%C3%ADo), and the [Viñales Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vi%C3%B1ales_Valley), was identified as early as the seventeenth century as the area producing the finest [Havana cigars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Habano_cigar?action=edit\&redlink=1) in the world, owing to the singular composition of its red soil (*tierra prieta*) and its specific climatic conditions.[^cite_note-Ortiz2002-297] The [cultural landscape](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_landscape) of the Viñales Valley was designated a [World Heritage Site](44940.md) by [UNESCO](21786641.md) in 1999.[^cite_note-UNESCOVinales-298]

The *vega*—the tobacco plantation—and the figure of the [veguero](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Veguero?action=edit\&redlink=1) (tobacco grower) gave rise to a distinct peasant culture in western Cuba.[^cite_note-Ortiz2002-297] The hand-rolling of the [habano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Habano_cigar?action=edit\&redlink=1)—the Cuban cigar—attained a singular level of artisanal refinement: the different components of the cigar (*capa*, *capote*, and *tripa*) require leaves with characteristics specific to each farm, and the craft of the [torcedor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torcedor) (cigar roller) demands years of training and practice. Among the most prestigious historic brands are [H. Upmann](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/H._Upmann) (founded 1844),[^cite_note-HUpmann-299] [Partagás](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partag%C3%A1s) (1845),[^cite_note-Partagas-300] [Romeo y Julieta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romeo_y_Julieta_\(cigar\)) (1875),[^cite_note-RyJ-301] and [Montecristo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montecristo_\(cigar\)) (1935),[^cite_note-Montecristo-302] all of which consolidated their reputations during the [Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_Cuba_\(1902%E2%80%931959\)). The [Cohiba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cohiba_\(cigar_brand\)) brand was created in 1966 for use in Cuban state protocol.[^cite_note-Cohiba-303]

In the cigar factories—the large hand-rolling workshops—there operated from the mid-nineteenth century onward a cultural institution with no documented equivalent in any other industry in the world: the [factory reader](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lector_de_tabaquer%C3%ADa?action=edit\&redlink=1). From 1865, when the practice was formalized with the support of labor leader [Saturnino Martínez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saturnino_Mart%C3%ADnez) in [Havana](49719.md),[^cite_note-Tinajero2007-304] the tobacco workers themselves paid out of their own pockets for a reader who, seated on an elevated platform at the center of the workshop, read aloud while they worked: newspapers, novels, plays, philosophical essays, and works of political theory.[^cite_note-Tinajero2007-304] The practice contributed to the intellectual and political development of the tobacco-working class, to the spread of abolitionist and pro-independence ideas, and produced a working class of notably high cultural attainment.[^cite_note-Tinajero2007-304] Cuba has put forward to UNESCO the nomination of this tradition for possible inscription on the [Representative List of the Intangible Cultural Heritage of Humanity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Representative_List_of_the_Intangible_Cultural_Heritage_of_Humanity).[^cite_note-UNESCOlectores-305]

#### Music

Music is one of the most internationally influential expressions of Cuban culture. It showcases the blend of European and African traditions that characterizes Cuban culture.[^cite_note-Carpentier1946-306][^cite_note-Sublette2007-307]

The oldest documented musical forms on the island include peasant songs of Spanish origin – romances, glossed décimas, tonadas – which coexisted with the ritual drumming and chants of African slaves. From this Spanish peasant substratum emerged the *punto guajiro*, or *punto cubano*, a vocal form of poetic improvisation in eight-syllable [décimas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/D%C3%A9cima), whose practice survives through *[repentismo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Repentismo?action=edit\&redlink=1)*, the art of improvised, extemporaneous versification. The [décima espinela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/D%C3%A9cima) is the quintessential stanzaic form of Cuban popular poetry.[^cite_note-Carpentier1946-306]

The [Cuban contradanza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Contradanza), derived from the French [Contredanse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Contra_dance) and the Spanish contradanza, became distinctly Cuban in the late eighteenth century through the incorporation of African syncopation and the rhythmic cell known as the [clave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clave_\(rhythm\)), thereby articulating the two principal axes of all subsequent Cuban music: melody of European origin and rhythm of African origin.[^cite_note-Sublette2007-307][^cite_note-Carpentier1946-306] The [habanera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Habanera_\(music\)), a genre that achieved enormous international popularity in the nineteenth century, developed from the contradanza; the composer [Sebastián Yradier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sebasti%C3%A1n_Yradier) used the habanera pattern in "[La paloma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Paloma_\(song\))" (c. 1861),[^cite_note-Sublette2007-307] and [Georges Bizet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georges_Bizet) employed it in the celebrated aria from *[Carmen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carmen_\(opera\))* (1875).

The [Danzón](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Danz%C3%B3n) evolved from the Cuban contradanza and was created by the Matanzas-born composer [Miguel Faílde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miguel_Fa%C3%ADlde), whose piece "Las alturas de Simpson" (1879) is regarded as the genre's first formally composed work.[^cite_note-Sublette2007-307][^cite_note-Manuel2006-308] The danzón was adopted as the national dance in the late nineteenth century and remained popular among the urban middle and working classes well into the twentieth century, being especially cultivated in the recreational societies of [Matanzas](1386915.md) and [Havana](49719.md).



Dancers of Cuban rumba in the Plaza de los Trabajadores, Camagüey, Cuba.

[Rumba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_rumba) emerged in the tenement courtyards (*solares*) and poor urban districts of Havana and Matanzas in the second half of the nineteenth century as a musical and choreographic expression of the working-class Afro-Cuban population. It should not be confused with Spanish [flamenco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flamenco) rumba, nor with the ballroom dance styles bearing that name abroad. Cuban rumba comprises three modalities: [Yambú](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yamb%C3%BA), slow in tempo and solemn in character; [columbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Columbia_\(dance\)?action=edit\&redlink=1), danced solo by men with great acrobatic virtuosity; and [Guaguancó](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guaguanc%C3%B3), the most widespread form, narrative and expressive in character, danced by both men and women. Its principal instruments are the [tumbadoras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conga_\(drum\)) (congas), [claves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clave_\(instrument\)?action=edit\&redlink=1), and cajones (box drums).[^cite_note-Sublette2007-307][^cite_note-Manuel2006-308] [UNESCO](21786641.md) inscribed rumba on the Representative List of the Intangible Cultural Heritage of Humanity in 2016.[^cite_note-UNESCO2016-309]

[Son cubano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Son_cubano), which would go on to exert a decisive influence on twentieth-century popular music of African descent throughout the Americas, developed in eastern Cuba – chiefly in what are now the provinces of [Santiago de Cuba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santiago_de_Cuba) and [Guantánamo](18952312.md) — in the late nineteenth century, out of the fusion of Spanish-derived *trova* singing with rhythmic percussion patterns of African origin, organized around the structure of the clave.[^cite_note-Sublette2007-307][^cite_note-Manuel2006-308] Its original instruments include the [tres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tres_\(instrument\)) — a three-course, six-string guitar with a distinctive timbre – along with [maracas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maracas), the [bongó](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bongo_drum), the [güiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/G%C3%BCiro), claves, and guitar; the trumpet was incorporated in later stages. Son moved from the eastern provinces to Havana in the early decades of the twentieth century and was widely disseminated by the [Sexteto Habanero](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sexteto_Habanero) (founded in 1920) and the [Septeto Nacional Ignacio Piñeiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Septeto_Nacional_Ignacio_Pi%C3%B1eiro?action=edit\&redlink=1) (founded in 1927), which made the genre's first successful recordings.[^cite_note-Sublette2007-307] The tres player and singer [Francisco Repilado, "Compay Segundo,"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Compay_Segundo) the composer [Miguel Matamoros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miguel_Matamoros), and the *sonero* [Nico Saquito](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nico_Saquito) are towering figures of classic son. Several subforms derived from the genre: [Son montuno](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Son_montuno), guajiro-son, [guaracha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guaracha) — festive and burlesque in character – and bolero-son.



[Pacho Alonso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pacho_Alonso), [La Lupe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Lupe), and [Beny Moré](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beny_Mor%C3%A9), three emblematic figures of twentieth-century Cuban popular music.
