Cuban [bolero](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolero), which emerged in Santiago de Cuba in the late nineteenth century as the sentimental branch of *trova*, differs from Spanish bolero in tempo, harmonic structure, and expression. Its creation is credited to the Santiago-born *trovador* [Pepe Sánchez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pepe_S%C3%A1nchez_\(musician\)), author of "Tristezas" (c. 1885), considered the genre's first composition.[^cite_note-Sublette2007-307][^cite_note-Manuel2006-308] It was cultivated within the tradition of *trova* by figures such as [Sindo Garay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sindo_Garay), [Alberto Villalón](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alberto_Villal%C3%B3n), and [Manuel Corona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manuel_Corona_\(musician\)), and in the twentieth century became the quintessential sentimental idiom of Spanish American popular music. [Beny Moré](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beny_Mor%C3%A9), [Elena Burke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elena_Burke), [Omara Portuondo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Omara_Portuondo), and [Ibrahim Ferrer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibrahim_Ferrer) represent its highest interpretive expressions.

[Afro-Cuban jazz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afro-Cuban_jazz) arose from the convergence of Cuban rhythms with American jazz beginning in the 1920s. The percussionist [Chano Pozo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chano_Pozo), who collaborated with [Dizzy Gillespie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dizzy_Gillespie) in New York from 1947 onward, and the trumpeter and bandleader [Mario Bauzá](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mario_Bauz%C3%A1), co-founder of the orchestra [Machito and His Afro-Cubans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Machito) in 1940, were decisive figures in the emergence of [Latin jazz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_jazz), or *cubop*.[^cite_note-Acosta2003-310] In Cuba, the [Orquesta Casino de la Playa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orquesta_Casino_de_la_Playa), the Orquesta Riverside, and the pianist [Bebo Valdés](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bebo_Vald%C3%A9s) developed a jazz idiom of their own, later continued by [Chucho Valdés](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chucho_Vald%C3%A9s) and the ensemble [Irakere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irakere) (founded in 1973).[^cite_note-Acosta2003-310]

[Mambo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mambo_\(music\)) emerged in the late 1930s and reached its height between the 1940s and 1950s, arising from the convergence of son with the [big band](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Big_band) format. The genre's paternity is disputed: the bassist [Orestes López](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orestes_L%C3%B3pez) and his brother [Israel "Cachao" López](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cachao) are credited as the authors of the danzón "Mambo" (1938), considered its direct antecedent; [Arsenio Rodríguez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenio_Rodr%C3%ADguez) contributed fundamental rhythmic innovations; and the pianist and bandleader [Dámaso Pérez Prado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/D%C3%A1maso_P%C3%A9rez_Prado), a Cuban born in Matanzas who settled in Mexico in 1948, propelled it to international fame with his recordings of the 1940s and 1950s.[^cite_note-Sublette2007-307][^cite_note-Manuel2006-308] The singer Beny Moré (1919–1963), regarded by much specialist criticism as the most accomplished Cuban musician of the twentieth century by virtue of his voice and exceptional stylistic versatility,[^cite_note-Sublette2007-307] was the foremost interpreter of mambo, son, and bolero in their most elaborate forms.

[Cha-cha-chá](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cha-cha-ch%C3%A1_\(music\)) was created around 1953 by the Havana-born violinist and bandleader [Enrique Jorrín](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enrique_Jorr%C3%ADn) as a rhythmic simplification of the danzón-mambo, intended to make the dance easier to follow.[^cite_note-Manuel2006-308][^cite_note-Sublette2007-307] Its name derives from the sound produced by dancers' feet performing the genre's characteristic offbeat step. It achieved immediate international popularity and became one of the most widely practiced [ballroom dances](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ballroom_dance) in the Western world during the second half of the twentieth century; the [Orquesta Aragón](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orquesta_Arag%C3%B3n) — founded in [Cienfuegos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cienfuegos) in 1939 – was its most representative ensemble.



[Silvio Rodríguez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silvio_Rodr%C3%ADguez), singer-songwriter and co-founder of the *nueva trova* movement, one of the most influential voices in Spanish-language song-writing.

[Nueva trova](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nueva_trova) emerged in the late 1960s as a singer-songwriter movement linked to the revolutionary process, albeit with a complex relationship to state cultural institutions.[^cite_note-Moore2006-311] Its principal figures, Silvio Rodríguez and [Pablo Milanés](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pablo_Milan%C3%A9s), developed a style that combined the Cuban *trova* tradition with American [folk music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Folk_music) and French [chanson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chanson), marked by texts of elaborate poetic construction. The [Grupo de Experimentación Sonora del ICAIC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grupo_de_Experimentaci%C3%B3n_Sonora_del_ICAIC?action=edit\&redlink=1), active between 1969 and 1978, served as the laboratory in which many of the movement's ideas took shape.[^cite_note-Moore2006-311] [Noel Nicola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noel_Nicola), [Sara González](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sara_Gonz%C3%A1lez_\(singer\)), and, in later generations, [Carlos Varela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carlos_Varela_\(musician\)?action=edit\&redlink=1) represent other strands of the movement.



[Pedrito Calvo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pedrito_Calvo), lead vocalist of Los Van Van for nearly thirty years and one of the most representative voices of Cuban timba.

[Timba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timba), developed in Havana from the late 1980s and consolidated during the 1990s, is the most influential contemporary expression of Cuban music. It combines son, rumba, jazz, funk, and the rhythmic patterns of the [batá](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bat%C3%A1_drum) drums within structures of great rhythmic and harmonic complexity, performed by large ensembles.[^cite_note-Perna2005-312][^cite_note-Moore2006-311] [Los Van Van](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Los_Van_Van) — founded by the bassist and composer [Juan Formell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_Formell) in 1969 – and [NG La Banda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NG_La_Banda) — founded by the flautist [José Luis Cortés](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Luis_Cort%C3%A9s_\(musician\)?action=edit\&redlink=1) in 1988 – are its most representative groups, along with [Issac Delgado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Issac_Delgado), [Bamboleo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bamboleo_\(band\)), and [Charanga Habanera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charanga_Habanera).[^cite_note-Perna2005-312]

The [Buena Vista Social Club](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buena_Vista_Social_Club) project (recorded in 1996; released in 1997 by [World Circuit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Circuit_Records?action=edit\&redlink=1)/[Nonesuch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nonesuch_Records)), coordinated by the American guitarist [Ry Cooder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ry_Cooder) together with producer [Nick Gold](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nick_Gold), brought together a group of Cuban musicians from earlier generations – among them [Compay Segundo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Compay_Segundo), Ibrahim Ferrer, [Rubén González](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rub%C3%A9n_Gonz%C3%A1lez_\(musician\)?action=edit\&redlink=1), and Omara Portuondo – and produced an album of enormous international impact that renewed worldwide interest in classic Cuban son.[^cite_note-Manuel2006-308][^cite_note-Perna2005-312] The eponymous documentary directed by [Wim Wenders](32902.md) (1999) further broadened its global reach.

#### Ballet

The antecedents of ballet in Cuba date to the 19th century, when the [Teatro Tacón](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gran_Teatro_de_La_Habana) in [Havana](49719.md) — officially inaugurated on 15 April 1838, and reputed in its day to be the largest and most lavish theater in the Americas, and the third in the world in technical and acoustic terms, after [La Scala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Scala) in Milan and the [Vienna State Opera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vienna_State_Opera)[^cite_note-313] — regularly hosted touring European opera and ballet companies. The founding of the [Sociedad Pro-Arte Musical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sociedad_Pro-Arte_Musical?action=edit\&redlink=1) on 2 December 1918, promoted by [María Teresa García Montes de Giberga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mar%C3%ADa_Teresa_Garc%C3%ADa_Montes_de_Giberga?action=edit\&redlink=1),[^cite_note-pacheco-314] created the institutional framework for the teaching and patronage of [classical ballet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_ballet) on the island, attracting European-trained teachers and training the first generations of Cuban dancers.



[Alicia Alonso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alicia_Alonso) (1920–2019), Cuban [prima ballerina assoluta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prima_ballerina_assoluta) and founder and director of the [National Ballet of Cuba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Ballet_of_Cuba), in 1958.

The central figure of Cuban ballet is [Alicia Alonso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alicia_Alonso) (1920–2019), a [prima ballerina assoluta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prima_ballerina_assoluta), choreographer, and teacher of international standing. Trained first in Havana and subsequently at the [School of American Ballet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/School_of_American_Ballet) in New York City, she joined [American Ballet Theatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Ballet_Theatre) in 1940,[^cite_note-singer-315] where she danced the leading roles of the classical repertoire — [Giselle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Giselle) in particular – despite progressively losing her sight from the late 1930s onward as a result of a bilateral retinal detachment.[^cite_note-obit-316] In October 1948 she founded, together with [Fernando Alonso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fernando_Alonso_\(dancer\)) and [Alberto Alonso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alberto_Alonso), the Ballet Alicia Alonso[^cite_note-singer-315] — renamed Ballet de Cuba in 1955 and, after the [Cuban Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_Revolution), reorganized in 1959 under its definitive name, the [National Ballet of Cuba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Ballet_of_Cuba).[^cite_note-bnchist-317] The company developed a technique of its own, known as the [Cuban school of ballet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_school_of_ballet?action=edit\&redlink=1), which combines the academic rigor of the Russian tradition with expressive elements particular to the Cuban dancer,[^cite_note-singer-315][^cite_note-318] and produced generations of internationally prominent artists. The National Ballet of Cuba received awards at festivals and competitions in Europe and the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union) from the 1960s onward – taking part in the [Varna International Ballet Competition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Varna_International_Ballet_Competition) for the first time in 1964 —[^cite_note-bnchist-317] and maintained a sustained international presence. Alonso continued to direct the company until her death on 17 October 2019, at the age of 98.[^cite_note-obit-316]

#### Literature

Main article: [Cuban literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_literature)

The oldest surviving text of literature produced in Cuba is the *[Espejo de paciencia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Espejo_de_paciencia)* (*Mirror of Patience*, 1608), an epic poem written in [ottava rima](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottava_rima) by [Silvestre de Balboa Troya y Quesada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silvestre_de_Balboa)—born in the [Canary Islands](5717.md) but resident on the island—recounting the kidnapping of Bishop [Juan de las Cabezas Altamirano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_de_las_Cabezas_Altamirano) by the French [privateer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Privateer) Gilberto Girón and his subsequent rescue.[^cite_note-vitier-319][^cite_note-chllal1-320] Rediscovered and published in 1838 by [José Antonio Echeverría](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Antonio_Echeverr%C3%ADa), the poem has been claimed as the symbolic starting point of Cuban letters, although Cuban literature as a distinct body of expression really began to take shape in the late colonial period, when a series of individual voices started to articulate a [Creole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Criollo_peoples) consciousness distinct from the Spanish metropole.

The first major name in Cuban letters is the [Romantic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanticism) poet [José María Heredia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Mar%C3%ADa_Heredia_\(Cuban_poet\)?action=edit\&redlink=1) (1803–1839), whose odes "En el Teocali de Cholula" and "[Niágara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oda_al_Ni%C3%A1gara?action=edit\&redlink=1)" place him among the precursors of [Spanish-language Romanticism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanticism_in_Hispanic_America?action=edit\&redlink=1) and among the first poets of continental renown.[^cite_note-chllal1-320] A contemporary of his, [Gertrudis Gómez de Avellaneda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gertrudis_G%C3%B3mez_de_Avellaneda) (1814–1873), was born in [Puerto Príncipe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puerto_Pr%C3%ADncipe,_Cuba)—present-day [Camagüey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camag%C3%BCey)—and pursued most of her career in Spain, but her novel *[Sab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sab_\(novel\))* (1841) stands as one of the earliest [abolitionist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abolitionism) texts written in Spanish, addressing [slavery in Cuba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavery_in_Cuba) from a critical standpoint unusual for its time.[^cite_note-321] Havana's slaveholding society found its most precise and enduring portrait in *[Cecilia Valdés](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cecilia_Vald%C3%A9s)* (definitive edition, 1882), by [Cirilo Villaverde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cirilo_Villaverde) (1812–1894), a central work of nineteenth-century Cuban [costumbrist realism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Costumbrismo) whose mixed-race protagonist became one of the most persistent symbols of national identity.[^cite_note-chllal1-320]

No figure, however, carries the weight in Cuban culture of [José Martí](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Mart%C3%AD) (1853–1895), whose importance transcends literature to encompass politics, ethical thought, and the very formation of the nation. As a writer, Martí renewed Spanish American prose and poetry with works such as *[Ismaelillo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ismaelillo?action=edit\&redlink=1)* (1882) and *[Versos sencillos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Versos_sencillos)* (1891), along with an extensive body of essays of extraordinary stylistic density, placing him among the founders of Spanish-language [Modernismo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modernismo).[^cite_note-322][^cite_note-chllal1-320] His death in combat at [Dos Ríos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Dos_R%C3%ADos) on 19 May 1895, during the [Cuban War of Independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_War_of_Independence), cemented his status as an irreplaceable founding figure.

In the early decades of the twentieth century, the poetic movement known as [Negrismo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Negrismo?action=edit\&redlink=1) reclaimed African heritage and popular [Afro-Cuban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afro-Cuban) culture as central components of national identity. Its foremost exponent was [Nicolás Guillén](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicol%C3%A1s_Guill%C3%A9n) (1902–1989), who in collections such as *[Motivos de son](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Motivos_de_son?action=edit\&redlink=1)* (1930), *[Sóngoro cosongo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S%C3%B3ngoro_cosongo?action=edit\&redlink=1)* (1931), and *[West Indies, Ltd.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Indies,_Ltd.?action=edit\&redlink=1)* (1934) incorporated the rhythms of [son](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Son_cubano) and [rumba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rumba), popular Afro-Cuban speech, and a critical stance toward colonialism and racism, becoming a leading figure in twentieth-century Spanish American poetry and [National Poet of Cuba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Poet_of_Cuba?action=edit\&redlink=1) after the [Cuban Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_Revolution) of 1959.[^cite_note-323] Those same years saw the emergence of [Alejo Carpentier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alejo_Carpentier) (1904–1980), a novelist, musicologist, and essayist born in [Havana](49719.md) to a French father and a Russian mother, who would become the most influential Cuban novelist in twentieth-century world literature. In the preface to his novel *[The Kingdom of This World](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Kingdom_of_This_World)* (1949), set during the [Haitian Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haitian_Revolution) led by [Toussaint Louverture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toussaint_Louverture), he formulated the concept of *[lo real maravilloso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lo_real_maravilloso)* ("the marvelous real"), a direct precursor of the [magical realism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magic_realism) that later flourished in Spanish America; his novels *[The Lost Steps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Lost_Steps)* (1953), *[Explosion in a Cathedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Explosion_in_a_Cathedral)* (1962), and *[Reasons of State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reasons_of_State)* (1974) combine historical and musicological erudition with an extraordinarily dense [Neobaroque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neobaroque) prose style.[^cite_note-324]



[José Lezama Lima](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Lezama_Lima), [Luis Rogelio Nogueras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luis_Rogelio_Nogueras?action=edit\&redlink=1), [Antón Arrufat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ant%C3%B3n_Arrufat), [Pablo Armando Fernández](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pablo_Armando_Fern%C3%A1ndez), [Mariano Rodríguez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mariano_Rodr%C3%ADguez_\(painter\)?action=edit\&redlink=1), [Heberto Padilla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heberto_Padilla), [Sigifredo Álvarez Conesa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sigifredo_%C3%81lvarez_Conesa?action=edit\&redlink=1), [Roberto Fernández Retamar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roberto_Fern%C3%A1ndez_Retamar), and [Víctor Casaus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/V%C3%ADctor_Casaus?action=edit\&redlink=1), young poets and writers, Havana, c. 1950.

The mid-century cultural renewal found its most radical figure in [José Lezama Lima](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Lezama_Lima) (1910–1976). A Havana-born poet, essayist, and novelist, Lezama founded and edited the literary magazine *[Orígenes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Or%C3%ADgenes_\(magazine\)?action=edit\&redlink=1)* (1944–1956), the principal hub of the Cuban cultural [avant-garde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avant-garde) of the period. His novel *[Paradiso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paradiso_\(novel\))* (1966), with its labyrinthine structure and extremely dense neobaroque prose, ranks among the most ambitious and demanding works in Spanish-language literature.[^cite_note-325] Close to him in sensibility but radically different in register, [Virgilio Piñera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virgilio_Pi%C3%B1era) (1912–1979)—primarily a playwright, though also a fiction writer and poet—represents the [absurdist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theatre_of_the_absurd) and [existentialist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Existentialism) strand of twentieth-century Cuban letters, producing a highly original body of work that endured periods of marginalization and official censorship during his lifetime.[^cite_note-chllal2-326]

The [Revolution of 1959](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_Revolution) produced a deep rupture in the Cuban literary field, dividing its principal voices between the island and exile. From London, where he settled permanently in 1966, [Guillermo Cabrera Infante](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guillermo_Cabrera_Infante) (1929–2005) published *[Three Trapped Tigers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Three_Trapped_Tigers)* (*Tres tristes tigres*, 1967), a novel that reconstructs the nocturnal atmosphere of pre-revolutionary [Havana](49719.md) through enormously inventive verbal play, indebted at once to [James Joyce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Joyce) and to Havana colloquial speech.[^cite_note-chllal2-326] [Reinaldo Arenas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reinaldo_Arenas) (1943–1990), persecuted by the state on account of his homosexuality and exiled from 1980, left behind a body of fiction of great intensity, the outstanding work being his autobiography *[Before Night Falls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Before_Night_Falls)* (*Antes que anochezca*), published posthumously in 1992 and adapted into a film in 2000.[^cite_note-327]



Leonardo Padura in 2015.

Contemporary Cuban literature finds its figure of greatest international reach in [Leonardo Padura](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leonardo_Padura) (b. 1955). Still resident in Cuba, Padura built, through his series of [noir crime novels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crime_fiction) featuring the detective [Mario Conde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mario_Conde_\(character\)?action=edit\&redlink=1)—among them *[Havana Red](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Havana_Red?action=edit\&redlink=1)* (*Máscaras*, 1997) and *[Havana Fever](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Havana_Fever?action=edit\&redlink=1)* (*La neblina del ayer*, 2005)—a literary chronicle of [Havana](49719.md) and of post-revolutionary Cuban society marked by notable critical depth. His novel *[The Man Who Loved Dogs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Man_Who_Loved_Dogs)* (*El hombre que amaba a los perros*, 2009), which reconstructs the final years of [Leon Trotsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leon_Trotsky) and the figure of his assassin [Ramón Mercader](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ram%C3%B3n_Mercader), won international acclaim. Padura received the [National Prize for Literature of Cuba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Prize_for_Literature_\(Cuba\)) in 2012[^cite_note-328] and the [Princess of Asturias Award for Literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Princess_of_Asturias_Award_for_Literature?action=edit\&redlink=1) in 2015.[^cite_note-329]

#### Visual arts

Cuban visual art has its roots in the academic painting of the colonial period, cultivated at the [San Alejandro National Academy of Fine Arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Alejandro_National_Academy_of_Fine_Arts?action=edit\&redlink=1), founded in [Havana](49719.md) on 12 January 1818, and generally regarded as the oldest continuously operating art school in Latin America.[^cite_note-330][^cite_note-331] Among the academic painters of the 19th and early 20th centuries who passed through its studios were [Leopoldo Romañach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leopoldo_Roma%C3%B1ach) and [Armando García Menocal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armando_Menocal).[^cite_note-332]



[Amelia Peláez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amelia_Pel%C3%A1ez) (1896–1968), a Cuban painter and a central figure of the island's artistic avant-garde.

The Cuban [avant-garde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avant-garde) emerged in the 1920s, when painters trained in Europe returned to the island with a renewed outlook. [Amelia Peláez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amelia_Pel%C3%A1ez) (1896–1968) developed an original pictorial idiom that fused [Cubism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cubism) and [Fauvism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fauvism) with the decorative motifs of Havana's colonial architecture—leaded stained glass, wrought-iron grilles and polychrome [azulejo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azulejo) tiles—in compositions of vivid color and pronounced geometric structure.[^cite_note-333] [Eduardo Abela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eduardo_Abela) (1889–1965) is remembered for his paintings of rural subjects and for creating the satirical character "El Bobo", which was widely reproduced in the press of the day.[^cite_note-334]



[Wifredo Lam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wifredo_Lam) (1902–1982), whose work brought together European [Surrealism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surrealism) and the iconography of Yoruba religion and Afro-Caribbean culture.

[Wifredo Lam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wifredo_Lam) (1902–1982), born in [Sagua la Grande](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sagua_la_Grande) to a father of Chinese descent and an [Afro-Cuban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afro-Cuban) mother, is the Cuban visual artist of greatest international standing of the 20th century. Trained in [Madrid](41188263.md) and formed within the Parisian [Surrealist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surrealism) circle—with close ties to [Pablo Picasso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pablo_Picasso) and [André Breton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andr%C3%A9_Breton)—he developed a pictorial vocabulary that brought together European Surrealism and the iconography of [Yoruba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yoruba_religion) worship and Afro-Caribbean culture.[^cite_note-335] His painting *The Jungle* (*La jungla*, 1942–1943) is part of the permanent collection of the Museum of Modern Art (MoMA) in New York.[^cite_note-336] [René Portocarrero](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ren%C3%A9_Portocarrero) (1912–1985) explored the iconography of Afro-Cuban religion and of colonial architecture with an exuberant palette and an expressionist lyricism of considerable intensity.[^cite_note-337]

After the [Cuban Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_Revolution) of 1959, the visual arts entered a period of ferment tied to the revolutionary cultural project, particularly evident in the political posters and graphic design of the [Cuban Institute of Cinematographic Art and Industry (ICAIC)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_Institute_of_Cinematographic_Art_and_Industry), which between the 1960s and the 1980s produced a school of poster design that won wide international recognition.[^cite_note-338] In January 1981 the group exhibition *Volumen Uno*, held at the Centro de Arte Internacional in Havana,[^cite_note-339] marked the emergence of a conceptually oriented generation of artists—among them [José Bedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Bedia_Vald%C3%A9s), [Flavio Garciandía](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flavio_Garciand%C3%ADa?action=edit\&redlink=1) and [Juan Francisco Elso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_Francisco_Elso)—who opened a dialogue with international currents in contemporary art.[^cite_note-340][^cite_note-Weiss2011-341] The collective [Los Carpinteros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Los_Carpinteros) (active from 1991; the name adopted in 1994)[^cite_note-342] gained international prominence in the 1990s with work combining humor, social criticism and the handcrafted fabrication of objects and imaginary architectures.[^cite_note-343] The painter [Tomás Sánchez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tom%C3%A1s_S%C3%A1nchez_\(painter\)?action=edit\&redlink=1) (born 1948) developed from the 1980s onward a [hyperrealist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hyperrealism_\(visual_arts\)) idiom of marked spirituality in his renderings of the Cuban landscape.[^cite_note-Weiss2011-341]

#### Cinema

The [cinematograph](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinematograph) arrived in Cuba on 24 January 1897, when the French technician [Gabriel Veyre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gabriel_Veyre), the [Lumière brothers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lumi%C3%A8re_brothers)' representative in Latin America, gave the first public screening at a venue on Prado Street 126 in [Havana](49719.md), barely thirteen months after the Lumières' first exhibition in Paris.[^cite_note-Chanan2004-344] Four European short films were shown at that inaugural session. On 7 February of the same year, Veyre filmed *Simulacro de incendio* (literally "Fire Drill")—a one-minute piece featuring Havana firefighters—regarded as the first film shot on Cuban soil, although the original print has been lost.[^cite_note-Chanan2004-344] The first Cuban to work in film projection and cinematography was José E. Casasús.

During the early decades of the 20th century, Cuban film production was scarce and aimed at popular consumption, dominated by newsreel documentaries and homemade comedies. [American cinema](418334.md) and, from the 1930s onward, [Mexican cinema](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinema_of_Mexico), dominated screens on the island. The domestic industry never developed a stable structure for production, distribution, or financing.[^cite_note-King2000-345]

The direct forerunner of modern Cuban cinema is *[El mégano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El_m%C3%A9gano?action=edit\&redlink=1)* (1955)—the title refers to a rural charcoal-burning encampment—a roughly twenty-five-minute documentary short co-directed by [Julio García Espinosa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Julio_Garc%C3%ADa_Espinosa) and [Tomás Gutiérrez Alea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tom%C3%A1s_Guti%C3%A9rrez_Alea), with the collaboration of [Alfredo Guevara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alfredo_Guevara_\(filmmaker\)?action=edit\&redlink=1) and José Massip, shot in the style of [Italian neorealism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_neorealism) among the charcoal workers of the [Zapata Swamp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zapata_Swamp), south of Havana.[^cite_note-Chanan2004_megano-346] The film exposed the living conditions of that working community under the government of [Fulgencio Batista](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fulgencio_Batista). Screened only twice—at the Varona Amphitheater of the [University of Havana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Havana) and at the Retiro Odontológico—it was immediately seized by the Cuban Military Intelligence Service (SIM), and García Espinosa was arrested. The print remained in SIM's archives until 1959. Both for its critical intent and for the profile of its makers, *El mégano* is regarded as the most important precursor of the Cuban New Cinema and one of the founding works of the [New Latin American Cinema](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Latin_American_Cinema?action=edit\&redlink=1) movement.[^cite_note-Chanan2004_megano-346][^cite_note-Paranagua2003-347]

The founding moment of modern Cuban cinema was the creation of the [Cuban Institute of Cinematographic Art and Industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_Institute_of_Cinematographic_Art_and_Industry) (ICAIC) on 24 March 1959, just weeks after the new government took power—reflecting the priority given to film as an instrument of cultural formation; the law establishing ICAIC was the first cultural measure enacted by the [Cuban Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_Revolution).[^cite_note-Chanan2004_ICAIC-348] [Alfredo Guevara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alfredo_Guevara_\(filmmaker\)?action=edit\&redlink=1) (1925–2013), a university classmate of [Fidel Castro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fidel_Castro) and one of the project's promoters, was appointed to head the institution, which he would lead, with one interruption, for roughly three decades.[^cite_note-Chanan2004_ICAIC-348] ICAIC's co-founders also included García Espinosa and Gutiérrez Alea, who had studied film direction at the [Centro Sperimentale di Cinematografia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centro_Sperimentale_di_Cinematografia) in Rome in the early 1950s. ICAIC concentrated within its structure a monopoly over film production, distribution, and exhibition in Cuba.

A decisive episode in defining the limits of artistic autonomy in revolutionary Cuba was the banning of the short film *[PM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PM_\(film\)?action=edit\&redlink=1)* (*Pasado Meridiano*, 1961), directed by [Sabá Cabrera Infante](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sab%C3%A1_Cabrera_Infante?action=edit\&redlink=1) and [Orlando Jiménez Leal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orlando_Jim%C3%A9nez_Leal?action=edit\&redlink=1). The fourteen-minute film recorded, using the observational technique associated with the British [Free Cinema](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_Cinema) movement, nightlife in the bars and taverns of the Havana waterfront. Although it had been broadcast on television without difficulty, ICAIC vetoed its release in theaters. The controversy sparked by the censorship led to a series of meetings between the revolutionary leadership and Cuban intellectuals, held in June 1961 at the [José Martí National Library](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Mart%C3%AD_National_Library?action=edit\&redlink=1). Closing the meetings, Fidel Castro delivered the address known as "[Words to the Intellectuals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Words_to_the_Intellectuals)," which laid out the guiding principle of revolutionary cultural policy in the formula: "Within the Revolution, everything; outside the Revolution, nothing."[^cite_note-349][^cite_note-Chanan2004_PM-350] This episode marked out the boundaries within which cinema—and the arts as a whole—would operate for the following decades, delimiting a space of relative creative freedom that permitted internal criticism but excluded dissent.

During the 1960s and 1970s, ICAIC produced films of notable artistic quality, recognized at European and Latin American festivals. [Julio García Espinosa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Julio_Garc%C3%ADa_Espinosa) (1926–2016), one of ICAIC's founders and director of *[Las aventuras de Juan Quinquín](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Las_aventuras_de_Juan_Quinqu%C3%ADn?action=edit\&redlink=1)* (1967), contributed to international theoretical debate with the influential formulation of his manifesto *[Por un cine imperfecto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Por_un_cine_imperfecto?action=edit\&redlink=1)* ("For an Imperfect Cinema," written in 1969, published in the Peruvian journal *Hablemos de cine* in 1970).[^cite_note-351] Against the technocratic aesthetic of [Hollywood](418334.md) and of certain strands of European art cinema, García Espinosa called for an "engaged" cinema, made with whatever means were available, oriented toward the spectator's active participation and in the service of social transformation. The text became a fundamental reference point in Latin American film theory and within the broader context of what came to be called [Third Cinema](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_Cinema).[^cite_note-King2000-345]

[Tomás Gutiérrez Alea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tom%C3%A1s_Guti%C3%A9rrez_Alea) (1928–1996), known as "Titón," is Cuban cinema's most acclaimed director. His films *[Memories of Underdevelopment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Memories_of_Underdevelopment)* (*Memorias del subdesarrollo*, 1968)—an adaptation of the novel of the same name by [Edmundo Desnoes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edmundo_Desnoes)—and *[Strawberry and Chocolate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strawberry_and_Chocolate)* (*Fresa y chocolate*, 1993, co-directed with [Juan Carlos Tabío](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_Carlos_Tab%C3%ADo)) are counted among the essential films of Latin American cinema.[^cite_note-Chanan2004_Alea-352] The former examines, with critical distance, the alienation of the Havana bourgeoisie during the revolution's early years; the latter was the first work of official Cuban cinema to address discrimination on grounds of [sexual orientation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sexual_orientation) within the revolutionary system, and it received a nomination for the [Academy Award for Best International Feature Film](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academy_Award_for_Best_International_Feature_Film) at the [67th Academy Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/67th_Academy_Awards) (1995).[^cite_note-353]

[Humberto Solás](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humberto_Sol%C3%A1s) (1941–2008) directed *[Lucía](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luc%C3%ADa_\(1968_film\)?action=edit\&redlink=1)* (1968), a film structured in three episodes set at different moments of Cuban history—1895, 1932, and the 1960s—which became a landmark of Latin American cinema and a reference point for feminist filmmaking.[^cite_note-Chanan2004_Alea-352] The documentarian [Santiago Álvarez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santiago_%C3%81lvarez_\(filmmaker\)) (1919–1998) renewed the language of documentary film with editing of great visual and political inventiveness, in works such as *[Now!](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Now!_\(short_film\)?action=edit\&redlink=1)* (1965) and *[LBJ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LBJ_\(short_film\)?action=edit\&redlink=1)* (1968).[^cite_note-Paranagua2003-347] [Manuel Octavio Gómez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manuel_Octavio_G%C3%B3mez) (1934–1988) directed *[La primera carga al machete](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_primera_carga_al_machete?action=edit\&redlink=1)* (1969), an experimental reconstruction of the nineteenth-century [wars of independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_Wars_of_Independence?action=edit\&redlink=1) shot in black and white using handheld-camera technique.

[Sara Gómez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sara_G%C3%B3mez) (1942–1974) was the first woman to direct films for the ICAIC and one of the institution's few filmmakers of African descent. Her documentary work addressed subjects including popular culture, racial prejudice, marginalization and changes associated with the Cuban Revolution. She later directed the feature film *[De cierta manera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/One_Way_or_Another_\(film\))* (1974), which combines documentary and fictional elements in its depiction of social change and conflicts between established attitudes and revolutionary ideals in a Havana neighborhood.[^cite_note-Chanan2004_Sara-354] Gómez died of an asthma attack before completing the films editing. The film was completed by [Gutiérrez Alea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tom%C3%A1s_Guti%C3%A9rrez_Alea) and [García Espinosa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Julio_Garc%C3%ADa_Espinosa) and released in 1977. Critics and scholars have discussed *De cierta manera* for its treatment of marginality, gender and race, and it has been examined within scholarship on [Latin American feminist cinema](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_American_feminist_cinema?action=edit\&redlink=1).[^cite_note-Chanan2004_Sara-354]

A figure of a very different order is [Nicolás Guillén Landrián](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicol%C3%A1s_Guill%C3%A9n_Landri%C3%A1n) (1938–2003), nephew of the poet [Nicolás Guillén](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicol%C3%A1s_Guill%C3%A9n). His experimental documentaries of the 1960s—among them *[Coffea Arábiga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coffea_Ar%C3%A1biga?action=edit\&redlink=1)* (1968) and *Taller de Línea y 18* (1971)—were marked by visual irony and disruptive editing that subverted the institutional commissions that had produced them. Subjected to repeated restrictions and to confinement in psychiatric institutions, where he underwent [electroconvulsive therapy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electroconvulsive_therapy), he was permanently expelled from ICAIC in 1971. After years of increasing marginalization, he went into exile in Miami in 1989, where he died in 2003. His filmography, ignored and censored for decades, was rediscovered beginning in the 2000s and is now recognized as one of the most original bodies of work in Latin American documentary film.[^cite_note-355]

The *[Noticiero ICAIC Latinoamericano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noticiero_ICAIC_Latinoamericano?action=edit\&redlink=1)*, produced weekly between 1960 and 1990 under the artistic direction of [Santiago Álvarez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santiago_%C3%81lvarez_\(filmmaker\)), served as a laboratory for audiovisual experimentation with no equivalent elsewhere in Latin American cinema of the period.[^cite_note-Paranagua2003-347] The [International Festival of New Latin American Cinema](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Havana_Film_Festival), founded in Havana in 1979 at the initiative of [Alfredo Guevara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alfredo_Guevara_\(filmmaker\)?action=edit\&redlink=1), became the leading forum for Latin American and Caribbean cinema. In 1986, the International School of Film and Television (Escuela Internacional de Cine y Televisión, EICTV) was founded in [San Antonio de los Baños](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Antonio_de_los_Ba%C3%B1os), a film training institution that has since welcomed students from around the world, with an orientation toward the cinemas of the [Global South](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_South).

Tension between cinema as art and as a political instrument surfaced with particular intensity in 1991, when the release of *[Alicia en el pueblo de Maravillas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alice_in_Wondertown)*, by [Daniel Díaz Torres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daniel_D%C3%ADaz_Torres)—a satire of bureaucracy and ideological dogmatism—triggered a political backlash that led the government to attempt to dissolve ICAIC by merging it with the Film Studios of the Revolutionary Armed Forces and the Cuban Institute of Radio and Television. Organized resistance from filmmakers and the cultural community prevented the dissolution, although ICAIC's director, [Julio García Espinosa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Julio_Garc%C3%ADa_Espinosa), was replaced by the returning [Alfredo Guevara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alfredo_Guevara_\(filmmaker\)?action=edit\&redlink=1).[^cite_note-Chanan2004_1991-356] The episode coincided with the collapse of the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union) and the onset of the so-called [Special Period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_Period), an economic crisis with devastating consequences for film production: ICAIC's financial resources contracted sharply, its equipment deteriorated, and annual feature-film output fell to minimal levels.



[Jorge Perugorría](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jorge_Perugorr%C3%ADa), Cuban actor, who played the role of Diego in *[Strawberry and Chocolate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strawberry_and_Chocolate)* (1993).

To sustain production, Cuban cinema increasingly turned to international [co-production](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Co-production_\(media\)), chiefly with Spain and Latin America, supported by the [Ibermedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibermedia?action=edit\&redlink=1) program. This model yielded works of significance but also generated friction between the commercial interests of foreign partners and the authorial logic of Cuban filmmaking. *[Fresa y chocolate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strawberry_and_Chocolate)* itself (1993), partly financed with international funds, was a paradigmatic product of this new production regime.[^cite_note-Stock2009-357]

The filmmaker who has most consistently articulated a view of Cuba from within this context of crisis is [Fernando Pérez Valdés](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fernando_P%C3%A9rez_\(director\)) (b. 1944), regarded by much of the critical establishment as the most important Cuban director of recent decades. His Special Period trilogy—*[Madagascar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madagascar_\(1994_film\))* (1994), *[La vida es silbar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Life_Is_to_Whistle)* (1998), and *[Suite Habana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suite_Habana)* (2003)—constitutes the most sustained and stylistically ambitious cinematic treatment of the Cuban experience of the 1990s.[^cite_note-Stock2009-357] *Madagascar* examines the deterioration of the relationship between a mother and her daughter against the backdrop of the crisis; *La vida es silbar* ("Life Is to Whistle"), more lyrical and dreamlike in register, won the [Coral Award](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coral_Award?action=edit\&redlink=1) for best direction at the [Havana Film Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Havana_Film_Festival) and was recognized at the [Sundance Film Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sundance_Film_Festival) and the [Berlin International Film Festival](18948039.md); *Suite Habana*, a dialogue-free documentary following thirteen residents of the capital over the course of a day, is considered by ICAIC itself the best Cuban documentary made since the institution's founding.[^cite_note-Stock2009-357]



Cuban actor [Héctor Noas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/H%C3%A9ctor_Noas?action=edit\&redlink=1), who appeared in *[Conducta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conducta_\(film\))* ("Behavior," 2014), directed by [Ernesto Daranas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ernesto_Daranas).

Twenty-first-century Cuban cinema has been marked by the emergence of filmmakers working outside or at the margins of ICAIC, the adoption of smaller formats and reduced budgets made possible by digital technology, and the growing presence of themes of emigration, marginality, and identity amid accelerating social change. *[Juan de los muertos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_de_los_muertos?action=edit\&redlink=1)* ("Juan of the Dead," 2011), by [Alejandro Brugués](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alejandro_Brugu%C3%A9s), the first Cuban horror film, laced with elements of political satire and shot as a Spanish–Cuban co-production, won the [Goya Award for Best Ibero-American Film](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goya_Award_for_Best_Ibero-American_Film) at the [27th Goya Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/27th_Goya_Awards) (2013) and renewed international attention for Cuban cinema.[^cite_note-358] [Ernesto Daranas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ernesto_Daranas), with *[Conducta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conducta_\(film\))* ("Behavior," 2014), addressed the contradictions of the Cuban educational system in a register of [social realism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_realism) that received wide national and international recognition.

The demand for a dedicated legal framework—a Film Law that would recognize independent production—has been a source of tension between Cuban filmmakers and the cultural administration since the mid-2000s, without such a law ever having been enacted.[^cite_note-359] Emigration has deprived island cinema of a significant number of filmmakers: exile has once again become, as it did for the generation of [Nicolás Guillén Landrián](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicol%C3%A1s_Guill%C3%A9n_Landri%C3%A1n), the path taken by many artists who found no conditions in Cuba to develop their work.

#### Sport



[Orestes Kindelán](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orestes_Kindel%C3%A1n), the Cuban ballplayer who is the all-time home run leader of the National Series with 487.

[Baseball](3850.md) is the [national sport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_sport) of [Cuba](5042481.md). Introduced to the island in the late 1860s by Cubans who had studied in the [United States](3434750.md),[^cite_note-gonzalez1999-360] it took root so deeply that the [Cuban League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_League), founded in 1878, is one of the oldest competitions of its kind anywhere in the world.[^cite_note-gonzalez1999-360][^cite_note-bjarkman2007-361] After 1959 Cuba built a high-performance sports system within a framework of official [amateurism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amateurism), which produced successive generations of first-rate players.[^cite_note-pettavino1994-362][^cite_note-bjarkman2007-361] The [national team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuba_national_baseball_team) won Olympic gold medals at [Barcelona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baseball_at_the_1992_Summer_Olympics) (1992), [Atlanta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baseball_at_the_1996_Summer_Olympics) (1996) and [Athens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baseball_at_the_2004_Summer_Olympics) (2004).[^cite_note-bjarkman2007-361][^cite_note-363] Players such as [third baseman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_baseman) [Omar Linares](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Omar_Linares), [center fielder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Center_fielder) [Víctor Mesa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/V%C3%ADctor_Mesa), [outfielder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Outfielder) [Orestes Kindelán](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orestes_Kindel%C3%A1n) and the [pitchers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pitcher) [Liván Hernández](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liv%C3%A1n_Hern%C3%A1ndez) and [Orlando "El Duque" Hernández](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orlando_Hern%C3%A1ndez) — half-brothers who later had successful careers in [Major League Baseball](38776.md) — are benchmarks of Cuban baseball at its highest level.[^cite_note-gonzalez1999-360][^cite_note-bjarkman2007-361]

Cuban [boxing](4243.md) came to dominate the international [amateur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amateur_boxing) circuit. [Teófilo Stevenson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Te%C3%B3filo_Stevenson) (1952–2012) won the Olympic [heavyweight](591011.md) title at [Munich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boxing_at_the_1972_Summer_Olympics) (1972), [Montreal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boxing_at_the_1976_Summer_Olympics) (1976) and [Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boxing_at_the_1980_Summer_Olympics) (1980), and is regarded by many specialists as the greatest amateur boxer of all time.[^cite_note-364][^cite_note-pettavino1994-362] [Félix Savón](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/F%C3%A9lix_Sav%C3%B3n) (b. 1967) repeated the feat in the same division at [Barcelona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boxing_at_the_1992_Summer_Olympics) (1992), [Atlanta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boxing_at_the_1996_Summer_Olympics) (1996) and [Sydney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boxing_at_the_2000_Summer_Olympics) (2000).[^cite_note-365] In the professional ranks, [Kid Chocolate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kid_Chocolate) ([Eligio Sardiñas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eligio_Sardi%C3%B1as?action=edit\&redlink=1), 1910–1988) became world [junior lightweight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Super_featherweight) champion in 1931, the first Cuban boxer to reach that level in the international professional game.[^cite_note-366] Women's [volleyball](32558.md), [athletics](194904.md) and [fencing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fencing) have also been high-performance disciplines in Cuban Olympic sport, reaching finals and podiums at the Games of the 1970s, 1980s and 1990s.[^cite_note-pettavino1994-362]

##### Chess



[José Raúl Capablanca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Ra%C3%BAl_Capablanca) in his youth, before winning the world title in 1921.

Cuba has a solid [chess](5134.md) tradition whose founding figure is [José Raúl Capablanca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Ra%C3%BAl_Capablanca), world champion from 1921 to 1927 and, to date, the only Latin American to have held the undisputed world title.[^cite_note-367] The [Capablanca Memorial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capablanca_Memorial), held in his honour since 1962, is one of the most prestigious international tournaments in the region.[^cite_note-368] Although its pool of registered players is far smaller than those of other Latin American countries — some 3,500 registered with the [International Chess Federation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FIDE) (FIDE), against more than 14,000 in Argentina and close to 27,000 in Brazil — Cuba has, according to FIDE figures, the largest number of active [grandmasters](12304.md) in Latin America (26, compared with 23 in Argentina and 15 in Brazil).[^cite_note-fide2024-369] This lead has held despite the departure of several of its leading figures to other federations, among them [Leinier Domínguez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leinier_Dom%C3%ADnguez) — winner of the World Blitz Championship in 2008 and a team silver medallist with the United States at the 2024 Budapest Olympiad — and [Lázaro Bruzón](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/L%C3%A1zaro_Bruz%C3%B3n) — world junior champion in 2000 — both of whom were trained entirely within the Cuban sports system before settling in the United States.[^cite_note-370][^cite_note-371] Individually, Cuban players have won eight medals across the history of the [Chess Olympiads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chess_Olympiad), beginning with the gold taken by Capablanca himself in 1939.[^cite_note-372]

#### Cuisine

[Cuban cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_cuisine) is the product of a convergence of [Spanish](26667.md), [African](5334607.md), [Chinese](5405.md) and [indigenous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_peoples_of_the_Caribbean) traditions.[^cite_note-folch2008-373][^cite_note-dawdy2002-374] It is marked by simple cooking techniques — [frying](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frying), [braising](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Braising) and slow stewing — by the prominence of animal protein and tropical [tubers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tuber), and by [sofrito](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sofrito), the slow frying of [garlic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Garlic), [onion](51258.md), [pepper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bell_pepper) and [tomato](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tomato), which serves as the aromatic base of most preparations.[^cite_note-vazquez2018-375][^cite_note-pelaez2014-376] Seasoning is moderate and the spices used are seldom hot; [olive oil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olive_oil) has coexisted with [lard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lard) as a cooking fat since the colonial period.[^cite_note-pelaez2014-376][^cite_note-folch2008-373]

[Ropa vieja](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ropa_vieja) — shredded [beef](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beef) cooked in tomato sauce with pepper and garlic — is one of the most emblematic dishes of Cuban cooking.[^cite_note-pelaez2014-376][^cite_note-vazquez2018-375] [Black beans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_turtle_bean), eaten either as a *potaje* or as an accompaniment to [rice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rice), are a fundamental element both nutritionally and symbolically, and are of African as much as Spanish inheritance.[^cite_note-dawdy2002-374][^cite_note-folch2008-373] [Moros y cristianos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moros_y_Cristianos_\(dish\)) — white rice and black beans cooked together — and eastern [congrí](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congr%C3%AD) — rice cooked with red beans, characteristic of [Oriente](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oriente_Province) — are ubiquitous side dishes on the Cuban table.[^cite_note-vazquez2018-375][^cite_note-garth2020-377] [Lechón asado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lechon) — a whole pig roasted over coals or in a *caja china* roasting box — is the festive preparation par excellence, above all at [Christmas Eve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nochebuena) celebrations.[^cite_note-pelaez2014-376][^cite_note-vazquez2018-375]

*Yuca con mojo* — boiled [cassava](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cassava) dressed with garlic fried in oil and [bitter orange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bitter_orange) juice — together with the [tostón](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tostones) — slices of green [plantain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plantain), flattened and fried — *mariquitas*, or thin plantain crisps, fried ripe plantain, [malanga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xanthosoma) and [boniato](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boniato), round out the repertoire of the most characteristic vegetable accompaniments.[^cite_note-vazquez2018-375][^cite_note-pelaez2014-376] Known collectively as *viandas*, these starches are treated as an indispensable component of an adequate meal, alongside rice, beans and a protein.[^cite_note-garth2020-377] All of these ingredients have [Indigenous American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_peoples_of_the_Americas) or African roots and came to be fully absorbed into the popular diet.[^cite_note-dawdy2002-374]

[Fernando Ortiz Fernández](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fernando_Ortiz_Fern%C3%A1ndez) raised the *ajiaco* — a stew of assorted tubers with various meats — to the status of a symbol of Cuban cultural synthesis, using it as a metaphor for [transculturation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transculturation): just as the stew fuses ingredients of disparate origin into a new and indivisible whole, so, on his account, did the formation of Cuban identity proceed.[^cite_note-378][^cite_note-379]

[Cuban coffee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_espresso), made from dark-roasted beans and served in small, highly concentrated measures — the [cortadito](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cortadito) in the cities, *café cerrero* in the countryside — is a social ritual of the first importance in daily life.[^cite_note-vazquez2018-375][^cite_note-garth2020-377] Cuban [rum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rum), obtained by distilling [sugarcane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sugarcane) products and ageing the spirit in [oak barrels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barrel), won worldwide renown through brands such as [Bacardi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bacardi) — founded in [Santiago de Cuba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santiago_de_Cuba) in 1862 by [Facundo Bacardí Massó](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Facundo_Bacard%C3%AD_Mass%C3%B3?action=edit\&redlink=1) and displaced from Cuba after 1959 —[^cite_note-gjelten2008-380] and [Havana Club](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Havana_Club) — founded in 1934 and today state-run, with international distribution through [Pernod Ricard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pernod_Ricard).[^cite_note-gjelten2008-380][^cite_note-wondrich2021-381] The classic [cocktails](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cocktail) associated with [Havana](49719.md), the [mojito](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mojito) and the [daiquiri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daiquiri), were popularised by the city's twentieth-century bar culture and are linked respectively to [La Bodeguita del Medio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Bodeguita_del_Medio) and [El Floridita](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El_Floridita),[^cite_note-wondrich2021-381] establishments whose international profile was boosted by the presence and the writings of [Ernest Hemingway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ernest_Hemingway) during his long residence in Cuba (1939–1960).[^cite_note-382]

## Religion

Main article: [Religion in Cuba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_Cuba)

Religion in Cuba (2010)

[^cite_note-Religion_in_Cuba-25]

1. [Catholicism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholicism) (60.0%)

2. [Protestantism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestantism) and other Christians (5.00%)

3. Others/African Religious (11.0%)

4. [Non-religious](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-religious) (24.0%)



The [Havana Cathedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Havana_Cathedral)

Cuba's prevailing religion is [Roman Catholicism](606848.md), although in some instances it is profoundly modified and influenced through [syncretism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syncretism). A common syncretic religion is [Santería](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santer%C3%ADa), which combined the [Yoruba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yoruba_people) religion of the African slaves with some Catholicism; it shows similarities to Brazilian [Umbanda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umbanda) and has been receiving a degree of official support.[^cite_note-383]

The [Roman Catholic Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Church) estimates that 60 percent of the population is Catholic,[^cite_note-StateDep-384] with 10 percent attending mass regularly,[^cite_note-385] while independent sources estimate that as few 1.5 percent of Catholics do so.[^cite_note-386]

Membership in [Protestant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestant) churches is estimated to be five percent and includes [Baptists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baptists), [Pentecostals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pentecostals), [Seventh-day Adventists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seventh-day_Adventists), [Presbyterians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presbyterians), Episcopal Church of Cuba|Episcopalians, [Methodists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Methodists), [Religious Society of Friends](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_Society_of_Friends) (Quakers), and [Lutherans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lutherans). Other groups include the [Greek Orthodox Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Orthodox_Church), the [Russian Orthodox Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Orthodox_Church), [Jehovah's Witnesses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jehovah's_Witnesses), [Hindus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindus), [Muslims](19541.md), [Buddhists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhists), [Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_Cuba), [Baháʼís](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bah%C3%A1%CA%BC%C3%AD_Faith), and [the Church of Jesus Christ of Latter-day Saints](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Church_of_Jesus_Christ_of_Latter-day_Saints).

Cuba is home to a variety of syncretic religions of largely African cultural origin. According to a US State Department report,[^cite_note-StateDep-384] some sources estimate that as much as 80 percent of the population consults with practitioners of religions with West African roots, such as Santeria or Yoruba. Santería developed out of the traditions of the [Yoruba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yoruba_people), one of the African peoples who were imported to Cuba during the 16th through 19th centuries to work on the sugar plantations. Santería blends elements of Christianity and [West African](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_African) beliefs and as such made it possible for the slaves to retain their traditional beliefs while appearing to practice Catholicism. La Virgen de la Caridad del Cobre (Our Lady of Charity) is the Catholic patroness of Cuba, and is greatly revered by the Cuban people and seen as a symbol of Cuba. In Santería, she has been syncretized with the goddess [Ochún](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Och%C3%BAn). The important religious festival "La Virgen de la Caridad del Cobre" is celebrated by Cubans annually on 8 September. Other religions practised are [Palo Monte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palo_Monte), and [Abakuá](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abaku%C3%A1), which have large parts of their liturgy in African languages.

### Symbols

The [flag of Cuba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flag_of_Cuba) is red, white, and blue; and was first adopted by [Narciso López](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Narciso_L%C3%B3pez) on a suggestion by the poet [Miguel Teurbe Tolón](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miguel_Teurbe_Tol%C3%B3n). The design incorporates three blue stripes, representing the three provinces of the time (Oriente, Centro, and Occidente), and two white stripes symbolizing the purity of the patriotic cause. The red triangle stands for the blood shed to free the nation. The white star in the triangle stands for independence.[^cite_note-387]



Coat of arms of Cuba

[^cite_note-143]: &#x20;The earliest mention of bullfighting in Cuba appears in Bartolomé de las Casas's [Historia de las Indias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historia_de_las_Indias), where a contest involving "a bull or bulls" is recorded as having taken place on [Corpus Christi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feast_of_Corpus_Christi) 1514. The first formal bullfight is generally dated to 1538, in [Santiago de Cuba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santiago_de_Cuba), on the occasion of the arrival of Governor [Hernando de Soto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hernando_de_Soto). This work should not be confused with the [Historia general y natural de las Indias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historia_general_y_natural_de_las_Indias) of [Gonzalo Fernández de Oviedo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gonzalo_Fern%C3%A1ndez_de_Oviedo).

Arrechea left the group in 2003.
